\section{\texorpdfstring{Task 5: Convergence of
\(\sqrt[n]{p}\)}{Task 5: Convergence of \textbackslash sqrt{[}n{]}\{p\}}}\label{task-5-convergence-of-sqrtnp}

Finally, we want to study the convergence behavior of
\(d_n = \sqrt[n]{p}\) for various \(p\). Set the parameters in the cell
below.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Set p}
\NormalTok{p }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{2}    \CommentTok{\# Experiment with different p}

\CommentTok{\# Set the range of n you want to consider}
\NormalTok{d\_start }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{1}
\NormalTok{d\_end }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{100}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ d(n):}
    \ControlFlowTok{return} \BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{(p}\OperatorTok{**}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{n))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

As above, to return just a single term of the sequence, use
\texttt{d(n)}:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{d(}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
1.4142135623730951
\end{verbatim}

Now let's plot the sequence:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{d\_array }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.arange(d\_start, d\_end)}
\NormalTok{d\_vals }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ p}\OperatorTok{**}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{d\_array)}
\NormalTok{view3 }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ pd.DataFrame(\{}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}n\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: d\_array, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}d\_n\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: d\_vals\})}
\CommentTok{\# display(view3)}

\NormalTok{plt.figure(figsize}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{8}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{plt.plot(view3[}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}n\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{], view3[}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}d\_n\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{], }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}.\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{, alpha}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{0.7}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{plt.xlabel(}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}n\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{plt.ylabel(}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}d\_n\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{plt.title(}\SpecialStringTok{f\textquotesingle{}Sequence n{-}th root of }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{p}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{plt.show()}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{notebooks/Week03_convergence_files/figure-pdf/cell-11-output-1.png}}

\textbf{Questions:} - Does this sequence converge for all \(p>0\)? - If
so, does the limit depend on \(p\)?

\chapter{Limits of Functions}\label{limits-of-functions}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, arc=.35mm, bottomrule=.15mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, colback=white, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, coltitle=black, left=2mm, leftrule=.75mm, opacityback=0, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, title={Companion reading}, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm]

This lecture corresponds to C\&J §1.8 (the concept of limit for a
function of a continuous variable), tying together §1.2.d (continuity)
with the Intermediate Value Theorem of §1.2.e.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{From Sequences to Functions}\label{from-sequences-to-functions}

We already know what it means for a \emph{sequence} to converge
(Definition~\ref{def-limit}). We now transfer this idea to functions:
instead of asking what happens to \(a_n\) as \(n\to\infty\), we ask what
happens to \(f(x)\) as \(x\) approaches some fixed point \(a\). The
cheapest way to make this precise is to reduce it to something we
already understand --- sequences --- by feeding \emph{every} sequence
approaching \(a\) into \(f\) and demanding that the outputs all converge
to the same value.

\begin{definition}[]\protect\hypertarget{def-function-limit}{}\label{def-function-limit}

\hfill\break
Let \(f\colon D\to\mathbb{R}\) with \(D\subseteq\mathbb{R}\), and let
\(a\in\mathbb{R}\). We say \[
\lim_{x\to a} f(x) = L
\] if for \textbf{every} sequence \((x_n)\) with \(x_n\in D\),
\(x_n\neq a\), and \(x_n\to a\), we have \(f(x_n)\to L\).

\end{definition}

\begin{refremark}
The word ``every'' is doing all the work: it is not enough to find
\emph{some} sequence \(x_n\to a\) with \(f(x_n)\to L\) --- the same
\(L\) must come out no matter which approaching sequence we feed in.
This mirrors uniqueness of sequential limits
(Remark~\ref{rem-limit-unique}): if two different sequences \(x_n\to a\)
and \(x_n'\to a\) produced different outputs \(f(x_n)\to L\) and
\(f(x_n')\to L'\neq L\), the limit \(\lim_{x\to a}f(x)\) would simply
not exist.

\label{rem-function-limit-every-sequence}

\end{refremark}

\begin{example}[]\protect\hypertarget{exm-x-squared-function-limit}{}\label{exm-x-squared-function-limit}

\hfill\break
Let \(f(x) = x^2\). Then \(\displaystyle\lim_{x\to 0} f(x) = 0\).

\begin{proof}
Let \((x_n)\) be an arbitrary sequence with \(x_n\to 0\). We may assume
\(\lvert x_n \rvert\le 1\) for all \(n\) (true eventually, since
\(x_n\to0\)). Then \[
0 \le f(x_n) = x_n^2 \le \lvert x_n \rvert,
\] and \(\lvert x_n \rvert\to 0\), so by the comparison test
(Proposition~\ref{prp-comparison}), \(x_n^2 \to 0\). Since \((x_n)\) was
an arbitrary sequence converging to \(0\),
Definition~\ref{def-function-limit} gives \(\lim_{x\to0} f(x) = 0\).
\end{proof}

\end{example}

\section{\texorpdfstring{The \(\varepsilon\)--\(\delta\)
Version}{The \textbackslash varepsilon--\textbackslash delta Version}}\label{the-varepsilondelta-version}

Just as with continuity (Definition~\ref{def-continuity}), the
sequential definition of a function limit has an equivalent
\(\varepsilon\)--\(\delta\) formulation.

\begin{proposition}[\(\varepsilon\)--\(\delta\) characterization of a
limit]\protect\hypertarget{prp-function-limit-eps-delta}{}\label{prp-function-limit-eps-delta}

Let \(f\colon D\to\mathbb{R}\), \(a\in\mathbb{R}\), \(L\in\mathbb{R}\).
Then \[
\lim_{x\to a} f(x) = L
\] if and only if \[
\text{for every } \varepsilon>0 \text{ there exists } \delta>0 \text{ s.t. }
x\in D,\ 0<\lvert x-a \rvert<\delta \quad \Rightarrow \quad\lvert f(x)-L \rvert<\varepsilon.
\]

\end{proposition}

The proof is the same idea as the equivalence used implicitly for
continuity: if the \(\varepsilon\)--\(\delta\) condition holds and
\(x_n\to a\), choosing \(N\) so that \(\lvert x_n-a \rvert<\delta\) for
\(n\ge N\) forces \(\lvert f(x_n)-L \rvert<\varepsilon\) for \(n\ge N\),
i.e.~\(f(x_n)\to L\). Conversely, if the \(\varepsilon\)--\(\delta\)
condition \emph{fails} for some \(\varepsilon>0\), then for every
\(\delta = \tfrac1n\) we can pick a point \(x_n\) with
\(0<\lvert x_n-a \rvert<\tfrac1n\) but
\(\lvert f(x_n)-L \rvert\ge\varepsilon\) --- producing a sequence
\(x_n\to a\) with \(f(x_n)\not\to L\), so the sequential definition
fails too.

The only difference from Definition~\ref{def-continuity} is the
punctured condition \(0<\lvert x-a \rvert\): a limit does not care what
happens (or fails to happen) \emph{at} \(a\) itself, only nearby. This
is what lets us connect the two notions.

\begin{proposition}[Continuity,
restated]\protect\hypertarget{prp-continuity-via-limit}{}\label{prp-continuity-via-limit}

Let \(f\colon D\to\mathbb{R}\) and \(a\in D\). Then \(f\) is continuous
at \(a\) (Definition~\ref{def-continuity}) if and only if \[
\lim_{x\to a} f(x) \text{ exists and equals } f(a).
\]

\end{proposition}

This is exactly Definition~\ref{def-continuity} with the roles of \(x\)
and \(a\) relabeled: continuity says
\(\lvert x-a \rvert<\delta \quad \Rightarrow \quad\lvert f(x)-f(a) \rvert<\varepsilon\),
which is the \(\varepsilon\)--\(\delta\) condition of
Proposition~\ref{prp-function-limit-eps-delta} with \(L=f(a)\) (the
point \(x=a\) itself trivially satisfies
\(\lvert f(x)-f(a) \rvert=0<\varepsilon\), so punctured and unpunctured
versions agree once \(L=f(a)\)).

\section{Examples}\label{examples}

\begin{example}[]\protect\hypertarget{exm-cube-continuous}{}\label{exm-cube-continuous}

\hfill\break
\(f(x) = x^3\) is continuous at every \(a\in\mathbb{R}\).

\begin{proof}
Let \(x_n\to a\). By the limit laws for sequences
(Proposition~\ref{prp-limit-laws}), \[
x_n^3 = x_n\cdot x_n\cdot x_n \longrightarrow a\cdot a\cdot a = a^3.
\] Hence \(f(x_n)\to f(a)\) for every sequence \(x_n\to a\), so
\(\lim_{x\to a} f(x) = f(a)\), and
Proposition~\ref{prp-continuity-via-limit} gives continuity at \(a\).
\end{proof}

\end{example}

\begin{example}[]\protect\hypertarget{exm-dirichlet-discontinuous}{}\label{exm-dirichlet-discontinuous}

\hfill\break
The \textbf{Dirichlet function} \[
f(x) = \begin{cases} 1 & x\in\mathbb{Q}\\ 0 & x\notin\mathbb{Q}\end{cases}
\] is discontinuous at every \(a\in\mathbb{R}\).

Take \(a=1\). Consider the two sequences \[
x_n = 1+\frac1n \quad(\text{rational}), \qquad\qquad y_n = 1+\frac{\sqrt2}{n} \quad(\text{irrational}).
\] Both converge to \(1\), but \[
f(x_n) \equiv 1 \to 1, \qquad\qquad f(y_n) \equiv 0 \to 0 \neq f(1) = 1.
\] Since two sequences converging to \(a=1\) produce different limits
for \(f\), \(\lim_{x\to1}f(x)\) does not exist
(Remark~\ref{rem-function-limit-every-sequence}), so \(f\) is not
continuous at \(1\).

\end{example}

\begin{exercise}[]\protect\hypertarget{exr-dirichlet-general-a}{}\label{exr-dirichlet-general-a}

Adapt the argument of Example~\ref{exm-dirichlet-discontinuous} to an
arbitrary \(a\in\mathbb{R}\): exhibit a rational sequence and an
irrational sequence, both converging to \(a\), on which the Dirichlet
function takes different limiting values. Conclude that \(f\) is
discontinuous at \emph{every} \(a\in\mathbb{R}\).

\end{exercise}

\begin{example}[]\protect\hypertarget{exm-piecewise-jump}{}\label{exm-piecewise-jump}

\hfill\break
Let \[
f(x) = \begin{cases} x^3+16 & x<0 \\ -2x+3 & x\ge 0. \end{cases}
\] Take \(x_n = -\tfrac1n \to 0\). Then \[
f(x_n) = \left(-\frac1n\right)^{\!3} + 16 \longrightarrow 16 \neq f(0) = 3,
\] so \(f\) is discontinuous at \(0\). We will see below exactly what
kind of discontinuity this is.

\end{example}

\section{One-Sided Limits}\label{one-sided-limits}

For Example~\ref{exm-piecewise-jump}, the failure of continuity comes
from the two ``branches'' of \(f\) disagreeing at \(0\) --- approaching
from the left gives a different answer than approaching from the right.
To capture this we restrict which sequences \(x_n\to a\) we are allowed
to test with.

\begin{definition}[]\protect\hypertarget{def-one-sided-limits}{}\label{def-one-sided-limits}

\hfill\break
Let \(f\colon D\to\mathbb{R}\) and \(a\in\mathbb{R}\).

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \(\displaystyle\lim_{x\to a^-} f(x) = L\) (the \textbf{left-sided
  limit}) if \(f(x_n)\to L\) for every \emph{increasing} sequence
  \(x_n \nearrow a\) with \(x_n\in D\).
\item
  \(\displaystyle\lim_{x\to a^+} f(x) = L\) (the \textbf{right-sided
  limit}) if \(f(x_n)\to L\) for every \emph{decreasing} sequence
  \(x_n \searrow a\) with \(x_n\in D\).
\end{itemize}

\end{definition}

\begin{refremark}
The left- and right-sided limits may disagree. For \(f\) as in
Example~\ref{exm-piecewise-jump}, \[
\lim_{x\to0^-} f(x) = 16, \qquad\qquad \lim_{x\to0^+} f(x) = 3,
\] since \(x^3+16\to16\) along any \(x_n\nearrow0\), while \(-2x+3\to3\)
along any \(x_n\searrow0\).

\label{rem-one-sided-disagree}

\end{refremark}

\begin{definition}[]\protect\hypertarget{def-jump-discontinuity}{}\label{def-jump-discontinuity}

\hfill\break
\(f\) has a \textbf{jump discontinuity} at \(a\) if both one-sided
limits exist and are finite, but \[
\lim_{x\to a^-} f(x) \neq \lim_{x\to a^+} f(x).
\] This makes precise the informal description from
Remark~\ref{rem-jump-discontinuity}, and excludes the unbounded case of
Remark~\ref{rem-infinite-discontinuity}, where no finite one-sided limit
exists at all. The function of Example~\ref{exm-piecewise-jump} has a
jump discontinuity at \(0\).

\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[]\protect\hypertarget{thm-continuity-one-sided}{}\label{thm-continuity-one-sided}

\hfill\break
Let \(f\colon D\to\mathbb{R}\) and \(a\in D\). Then \(f\) is continuous
at \(a\) if and only if \[
\lim_{x\to a^-} f(x) = f(a) = \lim_{x\to a^+} f(x).
\]

\end{theorem}

Like the two-sided limit
(Proposition~\ref{prp-function-limit-eps-delta}), each one-sided limit
has an equivalent \(\varepsilon\)--\(\delta\) form obtained by
restricting to one side: \[
\lim_{x\to a^-} f(x) = L \quad \Leftrightarrow \quad\forall\varepsilon>0\ \exists\delta>0:\ a-\delta<x<a \quad \Rightarrow \quad\lvert f(x)-L \rvert<\varepsilon,
\] and symmetrically for \(\lim_{x\to a^+}f(x)=L\) with
\(a<x<a+\delta\).

\begin{proof}
If \(f\) is continuous at \(a\), then \(f(x_n)\to f(a)\) for
\emph{every} sequence \(x_n\to a\)
(Proposition~\ref{prp-continuity-via-limit}) --- in particular for every
increasing sequence \(x_n\nearrow a\) and every decreasing sequence
\(x_n\searrow a\), giving both one-sided limits equal to \(f(a)\).

Conversely, suppose both one-sided limits equal \(f(a)\), and let
\(\varepsilon>0\). By the \(\varepsilon\)--\(\delta\) forms above there
exist \(\delta_1,\delta_2>0\) with \[
a-\delta_1<x<a \quad \Rightarrow \quad\lvert f(x)-f(a) \rvert<\varepsilon, \qquad\qquad a<x<a+\delta_2 \quad \Rightarrow \quad\lvert f(x)-f(a) \rvert<\varepsilon.
\] Let \(\delta=\min(\delta_1,\delta_2)\). If
\(0<\lvert x-a \rvert<\delta\), then either \(a-\delta<x<a\) or
\(a<x<a+\delta\), so in both cases
\(\lvert f(x)-f(a) \rvert<\varepsilon\); and \(x=a\) trivially gives
\(\lvert f(x)-f(a) \rvert=0<\varepsilon\). So
\(\lvert x-a \rvert<\delta \quad \Rightarrow \quad\lvert f(x)-f(a) \rvert<\varepsilon\),
which is exactly Definition~\ref{def-continuity}.
\end{proof}

\section{Limit Laws for Functions}\label{limit-laws-for-functions}

Because Definition~\ref{def-function-limit} reduces everything to
sequences, the arithmetic of function limits carries over directly from
the arithmetic of sequence limits (Proposition~\ref{prp-limit-laws}).

\begin{proposition}[Arithmetic of function
limits]\protect\hypertarget{prp-function-limit-laws}{}\label{prp-function-limit-laws}

Suppose \(\lim_{x\to a} f(x) = L\) and \(\lim_{x\to a} g(x) = M\). Then
\[
\lim_{x\to a} \big(f(x)+g(x)\big) = L+M, \qquad\qquad \lim_{x\to a} f(x)g(x) = LM,
\] and, if \(M\neq0\), \[
\lim_{x\to a} \frac{f(x)}{g(x)} = \frac{L}{M}.
\]

\end{proposition}

\begin{corollary}[]\protect\hypertarget{cor-continuity-algebra}{}\label{cor-continuity-algebra}

If \(a\in\operatorname{dom}(f)\cap\operatorname{dom}(g)\) and \(f,g\)
are continuous at \(a\), then \(f+g\), \(f\cdot g\), and (provided
\(g(a)\neq0\)) \(f/g\) are continuous at \(a\).

\end{corollary}

\begin{example}[]\protect\hypertarget{exm-rational-function-limit}{}\label{exm-rational-function-limit}

\hfill\break
\[
\lim_{x\to 0} \frac{x^3-3x+4}{x^2+\cos(x)}
= \frac{\lim_{x\to0}x^3 - \lim_{x\to0}3x + \lim_{x\to0}4}{\lim_{x\to0}x^2 + \lim_{x\to0}\cos(x)}
= \frac{0-0+4}{0+1} = 4.
\]

\end{example}

\begin{example}[]\protect\hypertarget{exm-quotient-careful}{}\label{exm-quotient-careful}

\hfill\break
\textbf{Careful:} the quotient rule requires the \emph{denominator's}
limit to be nonzero --- it says nothing when both numerator and
denominator vanish. For \[
\lim_{x\to a} \frac{x^2-a^2}{x-a},
\] we have \(\lim_{x\to a}(x-a) = 0\), so
Proposition~\ref{prp-function-limit-laws} simply does not apply.
Instead, factor first: \[
\frac{x^2-a^2}{x-a} = \frac{(x-a)(x+a)}{x-a} = x+a \qquad (x\neq a),
\] and only \emph{then} take the limit: \(\lim_{x\to a}(x+a) = 2a\).

\end{example}

\section{Continuity of Combinations and
Compositions}\label{continuity-of-combinations-and-compositions}

Proposition~\ref{prp-continuity-via-limit} lets us move freely between
``\(f\) is continuous at \(a\)'' and ``\(\lim_{x\to
a}f(x)=f(a)\)''. The next fact is the one we have already been using
informally whenever we wrote something like
\(\lim_{x\to a}\cos(h(x)) = \cos\!\big(\lim_{x\to a}h(x)\big)\): a limit
may be passed from the \emph{outside} of a continuous function to its
\emph{inside}.

\begin{proposition}[Limits pass through continuous
functions]\protect\hypertarget{prp-limit-inside-continuous}{}\label{prp-limit-inside-continuous}

Suppose \(\lim_{x\to a} f(x) = L\), and \(g\) is continuous at \(L\).
Then \[
\lim_{x\to a} g(f(x)) = g(L) = g\Big(\lim_{x\to a} f(x)\Big).
\]

\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Let \((x_n)\) be an arbitrary sequence with
\(x_n\in\operatorname{dom}(f)\), \(x_n\neq a\), \(x_n\to a\). By
hypothesis (Definition~\ref{def-function-limit}), \(f(x_n)\to L\). Since
\(g\) is continuous at \(L\), feeding the sequence
\(\big(f(x_n)\big)\to L\) into Definition~\ref{def-continuity} gives
\(g(f(x_n))\to g(L)\). As \((x_n)\) was an arbitrary sequence
approaching \(a\), Definition~\ref{def-function-limit} gives
\(\lim_{x\to a}g(f(x)) = g(L)\).
\end{proof}

Taking \(L=f(a)\) recovers continuity of a composition:

\begin{corollary}[Continuity of
compositions]\protect\hypertarget{cor-continuity-composition}{}\label{cor-continuity-composition}

If \(f\) is continuous at \(a\) and \(g\) is continuous at \(f(a)\),
then \(g\circ f\) is continuous at \(a\).

\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Since \(f\) is continuous at \(a\),
Proposition~\ref{prp-continuity-via-limit} gives
\(\lim_{x\to a}f(x) = f(a)\). Applying
Proposition~\ref{prp-limit-inside-continuous} with \(L=f(a)\), \[
\lim_{x\to a} (g\circ f)(x) = \lim_{x\to a} g(f(x)) = g(f(a)) = (g\circ f)(a),
\] so \(g\circ f\) is continuous at \(a\) by
Proposition~\ref{prp-continuity-via-limit}.
\end{proof}

Together with Corollary~\ref{cor-continuity-algebra} --- continuity of
\(f+g\), \(f\cdot g\), and \(f/g\) (where \(g\neq0\)) from continuity of
\(f\) and \(g\) --- Corollary~\ref{cor-continuity-composition} means
that \emph{any} function built out of continuous building blocks by
finitely many arithmetic operations and compositions is again continuous
wherever it is defined. This is exactly the justification behind phrases
like ``combination of continuous functions'' or ``composition of
continuous functions'' used to evaluate a limit by plugging in, as in
Example~\ref{exm-rational-function-limit} above.

\begin{refremark}
The \hyperref[the-elementary-functions]{elementary functions} ---
polynomials, rational functions, power and algebraic functions,
exponentials, logarithms, and the trigonometric functions --- are
continuous at every point of their domain. Each basic type can be
checked directly from Definition~\ref{def-continuity}, the way we
checked \(x^3\) in Example~\ref{exm-cube-continuous};
Corollary~\ref{cor-continuity-algebra} and
Corollary~\ref{cor-continuity-composition} then extend continuity to
every function assembled from these by arithmetic and composition, such
as \(\sqrt{2+\sqrt{2x}}\) or \(\cos\!\big(\pi\sin(x)/x\big)\). Once we
develop differentiation, this will also follow a second way: every
elementary function is differentiable wherever it is defined, and
differentiability will turn out to imply continuity.

\label{rem-elementary-continuous}

\end{refremark}

\section{The Intermediate Value
Theorem}\label{the-intermediate-value-theorem}

\begin{theorem}[Intermediate Value
Theorem]\protect\hypertarget{thm-ivt}{}\label{thm-ivt}

Let \(f\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}\) be continuous, and suppose
\(a<b\) with \(f(a)<0\) and \(f(b)>0\). Then there exists \(c\in(a,b)\)
with \(f(c)=0\).

\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
We construct \(c\) by \textbf{bisection}. Set \(a_1=a\), \(b_1=b\), so
\(f(a_1)\le0\le f(b_1)\). Given \(a_n,b_n\) with \(f(a_n)\le0\) and
\(f(b_n)\ge0\), let \(m_n = \tfrac{a_n+b_n}{2}\).

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  If \(f(m_n)=0\), we are done --- take \(c=m_n\).
\item
  If \(f(m_n)<0\), set \(a_{n+1}=m_n\), \(b_{n+1}=b_n\).
\item
  If \(f(m_n)>0\), set \(a_{n+1}=a_n\), \(b_{n+1}=m_n\).
\end{itemize}

In either of the last two cases \(f(a_{n+1})\le0\le f(b_{n+1})\), so the
invariant is preserved, and we continue. If the process never
terminates, this produces sequences with \((a_n)\) increasing, \((b_n)\)
decreasing, \(a_n\le b_n\) for all \(n\), and \[
b_n - a_n = \frac{b-a}{2^{n-1}} \longrightarrow 0
\] (the interval length halves at each step; compare
Exercise~\ref{exr-warmup-half-n}). By
Corollary~\ref{cor-monotone-squeeze}, \((a_n)\) and \((b_n)\) converge
to a common limit \(c\in[a,b]\).

Since \(f\) is continuous, Proposition~\ref{prp-continuity-via-limit}
gives \(f(a_n)\to f(c)\) and \(f(b_n)\to f(c)\). Now \(f(a_n)\le0\) for
every \(n\); if we had \(f(c)>0\), taking \(\varepsilon=f(c)/2\) in the
definition of convergence would force \(f(a_n)>f(c)/2>0\) for large
\(n\), a contradiction. So \(f(c)\le0\). The same argument with
\(f(b_n)\ge0\) gives \(f(c)\ge0\). Hence \(f(c)=0\), and \(a<c<b\) since
\(f(a)<0\) and \(f(b)>0\) rule out \(c=a\) or \(c=b\).
\end{proof}

\begin{refremark}
The same bisection idea proves the more general statement: if \(f\) is
continuous on \([a,b]\) and \(y\) lies between \(f(a)\) and \(f(b)\),
then \(f(c)=y\) for some \(c\in[a,b]\) --- apply Theorem~\ref{thm-ivt}
to \(f(x)-y\).

\label{rem-ivt-general}

\end{refremark}

\section{Summary}\label{summary-6}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \(\displaystyle\lim_{x\to a} f(x) = L\)
  (Definition~\ref{def-function-limit}) means \(f(x_n)\to L\) for
  \textbf{every} sequence \(x_n\to a\) in \(\operatorname{dom}(f)\) ---
  this reduces function limits to sequence limits, and is equivalent to
  the \(\varepsilon\)--\(\delta\) condition of
  Proposition~\ref{prp-function-limit-eps-delta}.
\item
  \textbf{Continuity restated:} \(f\) is continuous at \(a\) exactly
  when \(\lim_{x\to a}f(x)\) exists and equals \(f(a)\)
  (Proposition~\ref{prp-continuity-via-limit}).
\item
  \textbf{One-sided limits} (Definition~\ref{def-one-sided-limits}) test
  only monotone sequences approaching from one side. A \textbf{jump
  discontinuity} (Definition~\ref{def-jump-discontinuity}) is exactly a
  point where both one-sided limits exist (finitely) but disagree; \(f\)
  is continuous at \(a\) iff both one-sided limits equal \(f(a)\)
  (Theorem~\ref{thm-continuity-one-sided}).
\item
  The \textbf{limit laws} (Proposition~\ref{prp-function-limit-laws})
  compute limits of sums, products, and quotients termwise, and give
  continuity of \(f+g\), \(f\cdot g\), \(f/g\) from continuity of \(f\)
  and \(g\) (Corollary~\ref{cor-continuity-algebra}) --- but a
  \(\tfrac00\) form must be simplified \emph{before} the quotient rule
  applies (Example~\ref{exm-quotient-careful}).
\item
  \textbf{Compositions of continuous functions are continuous}
  (Corollary~\ref{cor-continuity-composition}), and more generally a
  limit may be passed through a continuous function from the outside in
  (Proposition~\ref{prp-limit-inside-continuous}). Together with the
  arithmetic rule above, this shows every \textbf{elementary function}
  is continuous where defined (Remark~\ref{rem-elementary-continuous}).
\item
  The \textbf{Intermediate Value Theorem} (Theorem~\ref{thm-ivt}): a
  continuous function that is negative at \(a\) and positive at \(b\)
  must vanish somewhere in between. The proof bisects \([a,b]\) into
  nested intervals shrinking to a point \(c\), then uses continuity to
  pass \(f(a_n)\le0\le f(b_n)\) to the limit.
\end{itemize}

\part{The Riemann Integral}

\chapter{Riemann Sums}\label{riemann-sums}

\section{Area of the Circle}\label{area-of-the-circle}

We start with a problem from antiquity: what is the area of a circle of
radius \(1\)? The classical idea (going back to Archimedes) is to
approximate the circle by figures whose area we \emph{can} compute ---
regular \(n\)-gons inscribed in the circle.

Split the \(n\)-th polygon (\(n\ge3\)) into \(n\) congruent triangles,
each with its apex at the center of the circle, two sides of length
\(1\), and apex angle \(\tfrac{2\pi}{n}\). Taking one of the two unit
sides as base, the height of the triangle is
\(h = \sin\!\big(\tfrac{2\pi}{n}\big)\), so \[
\text{area of one triangle} = \frac12\cdot 1\cdot h = \frac{\sin\!\big(\tfrac{2\pi}{n}\big)}{2},
\] \[
A_n := \text{area of the $n$-gon} = \frac{n}{2}\,\sin\!\Big(\frac{2\pi}{n}\Big).
\] As \(n\) grows, the polygons hug the circle more and more tightly,
and \(A_n\) should approximate the area of the circle better and better.

Formally, we can say two things about the sequence \((A_n)\):

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \((A_n)\) is \textbf{increasing}.
\item
  \((A_n)\) is \textbf{bounded}: every inscribed \(n\)-gon is contained
  in the square of side length \(2\) circumscribing the circle, so
  \(A_n\le 4\) for all \(n\).
\end{itemize}

By the Monotone Convergence Theorem (2), \((A_n)\) converges. Its limit
is \(\pi\).

\begin{refremark}
Two points are glossed over here. Monotonicity is not obvious from the
picture, since the \(n\)-gon is not contained in the \((n+1)\)-gon; it
follows from the fact that \(\sin(x)/x\) is decreasing on \((0,\pi]\),
writing \(A_n = \pi\cdot\dfrac{\sin(2\pi/n)}{2\pi/n}\). The same
rewriting shows that identifying the limit as \(\pi\) amounts to knowing
\(\lim_{x\to0}\frac{\sin x}{x} = 1\) --- a limit we will return to when
we study derivatives of the trigonometric functions.

\label{rem-circle-area-details}

\end{refremark}

How can we generalize this idea to other areas? Triangles could work,
but it is not clear how to set this up \emph{methodically} for an
arbitrary region. For regions under the graph of a function, there is a
better choice: \textbf{rectangles}, whose area is as easy to compute as
it gets.

\section{Approximation of Areas by
Rectangles}\label{approximation-of-areas-by-rectangles}

Whatever approximation scheme we use, we want it to have the following
properties:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  It uses a geometric figure whose area is easily computable. Rectangles
  are as easy as it gets.
\item
  There is a \textbf{general method} to approximate an area by
  rectangles.
\item
  There is a \textbf{general method} to \textbf{refine} any
  approximation.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Areas under Curves}\label{areas-under-curves}

Let \(f\colon[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}\) be continuous with \(f(x)\ge0\) for
all \(x\in[a,b]\). We want to compute \[
A := \text{area between the graph of $f$ and the $x$-axis, between $x=a$ and $x=b$}.
\]

The idea is as follows.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Partition} \([a,b]\) into finitely many segments \[
  a = x_0 < x_1 < \cdots < x_n = b.
  \] The segments \([x_{i-1},x_i]\) will form the \emph{bases} of
  rectangles.
\item
  For each segment \([x_{i-1},x_i]\), pick a point
  \(x_i^*\in[x_{i-1},x_i]\). The \emph{height} of the \(i\)-th rectangle
  is \(f(x_i^*)\).
\end{itemize}

If \(R_i\) denotes the area of the \(i\)-th rectangle, then \[
R_i = \lvert x_i - x_{i-1} \rvert\cdot f(x_i^*) \qquad (i=1,\ldots,n),
\] and \(R_1+\cdots+R_n\) is an approximation to \(A\).

\begin{definition}[]\protect\hypertarget{def-riemann-sum}{}\label{def-riemann-sum}

\hfill\break
A finite set of points \[
P:\quad a = x_0 < x_1 < \cdots < x_n = b
\] is called a \textbf{partition} of \([a,b]\). Points
\(S = \{x_1^*,\ldots,x_n^*\}\) with \(x_i^*\in[x_{i-1},x_i]\) are called
\textbf{sample points}, and \[
\Delta_i = \lvert x_i - x_{i-1} \rvert = x_i - x_{i-1} \qquad (i=1,\ldots,n)
\] is the length of the \(i\)-th segment. The \textbf{Riemann sum} for
\(f\) on \([a,b]\), given \(P\) and \(S\), is \[
RS(P,S) = f(x_1^*)\,\Delta_1 + f(x_2^*)\,\Delta_2 + \cdots + f(x_n^*)\,\Delta_n
= \sum_{i=1}^n f(x_i^*)\,\Delta_i .
\]

\end{definition}

The symbol \(\sum\) is \textbf{summation} (or \textbf{\(\Sigma\)-})
\textbf{notation}: \(\sum_{i=1}^n c_i\) stands for
\(c_1+c_2+\cdots+c_n\). Note that Definition~\ref{def-riemann-sum} makes
sense for \emph{any} function \(f\) on \([a,b]\) --- neither continuity
nor \(f\ge0\) is needed to \emph{form} the sum. (If \(f\) takes negative
values, the corresponding terms simply count as negative area.)

\textbf{Refinement.} How do we make the approximation better? We
\emph{refine the partition} --- add more division points so that the
segments get shorter --- and choose new sample points. The goal is to
find a way to do this that is both \emph{methodological} and
\emph{practical}. The natural requirement is that the length of
\textbf{every} segment tends to \(0\). It is convenient to measure this
by the length of the longest segment, \[
\|P\| := \max_{1\le i\le n}\Delta_i,
\] called the \textbf{mesh} of \(P\). (Other authors call it the
\emph{span}.)

\section{Equipartitions and the Definition of the
Integral}\label{equipartitions-and-the-definition-of-the-integral}

It turns out the approximation process is remarkably robust --- several
approaches are possible, and they all give the same answer. The most
practical one restricts to a special kind of partition.

\textbf{Equipartitions.} All segments have the \emph{same} length, so
only the number of segments matters. With \(n\) segments, \[
\Delta_i = \frac{b-a}{n} =: \Delta \quad\text{for all } i, \qquad\qquad x_i = a + i\Delta.
\] There are three standard ways to choose sample points:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{left endpoints:} \(x_i^* = x_{i-1}\),
\item
  \textbf{right endpoints:} \(x_i^* = x_i\),
\item
  \textbf{midpoints:} \(x_i^* = \bar x_i := \dfrac{x_{i-1}+x_i}{2}\).
\end{itemize}

This gives three sequences of Riemann sums: \[
LHS_n = \sum_{i=1}^n f(x_{i-1})\,\Delta, \qquad
RHS_n = \sum_{i=1}^n f(x_i)\,\Delta, \qquad
MS_n = \sum_{i=1}^n f(\bar x_i)\,\Delta,
\] the \textbf{left-hand sum}, the \textbf{right-hand sum}, and the
\textbf{midpoint sum}.

\begin{theorem}[]\protect\hypertarget{thm-riemann-equipartition}{}\label{thm-riemann-equipartition}

\hfill\break
If \(f\) is continuous on \([a,b]\), then all three limits \[
\lim_{n\to\infty} LHS_n, \qquad \lim_{n\to\infty} RHS_n, \qquad \lim_{n\to\infty} MS_n
\] exist and are equal.

\end{theorem}

\begin{definition}[]\protect\hypertarget{def-riemann-integral}{}\label{def-riemann-integral}

\hfill\break
Let \(f\) be continuous on \([a,b]\). The \textbf{(Riemann) integral} of
\(f\) from \(a\) to \(b\) is the common value \(L\) of the limits in
Theorem~\ref{thm-riemann-equipartition}: \[
\int_a^b f(x)\,dx = L = \lim_{n\to\infty} LHS_n = \lim_{n\to\infty} RHS_n = \lim_{n\to\infty} MS_n.
\]

\end{definition}

The notation is Leibniz's: the integral sign \(\int\) is an elongated S
for ``sum'', and \(dx\) recalls the lengths \(\Delta\). It is only
notation --- \(dx\) on its own has no meaning here. Likewise, the name
of the variable is irrelevant:
\(\int_a^b f(x)\,dx = \int_a^b f(t)\,dt\).

\section{Why the Choice Does Not Matter
(optional)}\label{sec-choice-does-not-matter}

Theorem~\ref{thm-riemann-equipartition} is a special case of a much
stronger statement: for a continuous function it does not matter at all
\emph{how} we partition or \emph{where} we sample, as long as the
segments get uniformly short.

\begin{theorem}[Existence of the
integral]\protect\hypertarget{thm-riemann-general}{}\label{thm-riemann-general}

Let \(f\) be continuous on \([a,b]\). There is a unique number \(I\)
with the following property: for \textbf{every} sequence of partitions
\(P_1,P_2,\ldots\) of \([a,b]\) with \(\|P_n\|\to0\), and \textbf{every}
choice of sample points \(S_n\) for \(P_n\), \[
\lim_{n\to\infty} RS(P_n,S_n) = I.
\]

\end{theorem}

Since an equipartition into \(n\) segments has mesh
\(\tfrac{b-a}{n}\to0\), Theorem~\ref{thm-riemann-equipartition} follows
at once, with \(\int_a^b f(x)\,dx = I\). We now sketch \emph{why}
Theorem~\ref{thm-riemann-general} is true. The argument is informal in
two places, flagged in Remark~\ref{rem-darboux-informal} below.

\subsection{Step 1: Trapping Every Riemann
Sum}\label{step-1-trapping-every-riemann-sum}

A continuous function on a closed interval attains a maximum and a
minimum there. (This is the \emph{Extreme Value Theorem}, which we have
not proved yet.) So for a partition \(P\) we can put \[
m_i = \min\{f(x) : x_{i-1}\le x\le x_i\}, \qquad
M_i = \max\{f(x) : x_{i-1}\le x\le x_i\},
\] and form the \textbf{lower} and \textbf{upper (Darboux) sums} \[
L(P) = \sum_{i=1}^n m_i\,\Delta_i, \qquad\qquad U(P) = \sum_{i=1}^n M_i\,\Delta_i.
\] Geometrically (for \(f\ge0\)), \(L(P)\) is the area of the largest
rectangles on the segments that fit \emph{under} the graph, and \(U(P)\)
is the area of the smallest rectangles that reach \emph{over} it.

Whatever sample points \(x_i^*\) we choose, \(m_i\le f(x_i^*)\le M_i\),
so \[
L(P) \;\le\; RS(P,S) \;\le\; U(P) \qquad\text{for every choice of sample points } S.
\] In other words, \(L(P)\) and \(U(P)\) are the \emph{worst cases} over
all choices of sample points. If we can show that they are close to each
other, the choice of sample points becomes irrelevant.

\subsection{Step 2: Refining Moves the Sums Toward Each
Other}\label{step-2-refining-moves-the-sums-toward-each-other}

Suppose \(P'\) is \textbf{finer} than \(P\), i.e.~\(P'\) contains all
points of \(P\) and possibly more. Adding a single point \(z\) inside a
segment \([x_{i-1},x_i]\) splits it into \([x_{i-1},z]\) and
\([z,x_i]\). The minimum of \(f\) over each smaller piece is \emph{at
least} the minimum \(m_i\) over the whole segment, and the maximum over
each piece is \emph{at most} \(M_i\). Adding points one at a time, we
get \[
L(P) \le L(P') \qquad\text{and}\qquad U(P') \le U(P).
\] So refining can only push the lower sum up and the upper sum down.

This has a useful consequence. Given \emph{any} two partitions \(P\) and
\(Q\), the partition \(P\cup Q\) is finer than both, so \[
L(P) \;\le\; L(P\cup Q) \;\le\; U(P\cup Q) \;\le\; U(Q).
\] That is: \textbf{every lower sum is at most every upper sum}, no
matter which partitions they come from. All lower sums sit to the left
of all upper sums on the number line, and by the completeness of
\(\mathbb{R}\) (compare the Nested Interval Axiom) there is at least one
number \(I\) in between: \[
L(P) \le I \le U(Q) \qquad\text{for all partitions } P, Q.
\]

\subsection{Step 3: Closing the Gap}\label{step-3-closing-the-gap}

The gap between upper and lower sum is \[
U(P) - L(P) = \sum_{i=1}^n (M_i - m_i)\,\Delta_i.
\] Here continuity enters. On a very short segment a continuous function
cannot vary much. Suppose we know the following: for every
\(\varepsilon>0\) there is a \(\delta>0\) such that
\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-uniform-continuity}{
\lvert x-y \rvert<\delta \quad \Rightarrow \quad\lvert f(x)-f(y) \rvert<\varepsilon\qquad\text{for all } x,y\in[a,b].
}\end{equation} Then, as soon as \(\|P\|<\delta\), any two points in the
same segment are within \(\delta\) of each other, so
\(M_i - m_i<\varepsilon\) for every \(i\), and \[
U(P) - L(P) < \sum_{i=1}^n \varepsilon\,\Delta_i = \varepsilon\,(b-a).
\] Since \(\varepsilon\) was arbitrary, the gap can be made as small as
we like by making the mesh small.

\subsection{Step 4: Conclusion}\label{step-4-conclusion}

Now let \(\|P_n\|\to0\) and let \(S_n\) be any sample points. By Steps 1
and 2, both \(RS(P_n,S_n)\) and \(I\) lie in the interval
\([L(P_n),U(P_n)]\), so \[
\lvert RS(P_n,S_n) - I \rvert \le U(P_n) - L(P_n).
\] By Step 3 the right-hand side tends to \(0\), so \(RS(P_n,S_n)\to I\)
(by the comparison test, Proposition~\ref{prp-comparison}). The number
\(I\) is unique: any other \(I'\) trapped between all lower and all
upper sums satisfies \(\lvert I-I' \rvert\le U(P_n)-L(P_n)\to0\) as
well. Nothing in this argument depended on \emph{which} partitions or
sample points we used --- only on the mesh going to \(0\).

\begin{refremark}
The sketch uses three facts we have not yet established:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  the \textbf{Extreme Value Theorem} (continuous functions attain a max
  and min on closed intervals), used to define \(m_i\) and \(M_i\);
\item
  the existence of a number \(I\) between all lower and all upper sums,
  which is a consequence of the completeness of \(\mathbb{R}\);
\item
  condition Equation~\ref{eq-uniform-continuity}, called \textbf{uniform
  continuity}.
\end{enumerate}

The third point is the subtle one. It looks like
Definition~\ref{def-continuity}, but the quantifiers are in a different
order: in Definition~\ref{def-continuity}, \(\delta\) is chosen
\emph{after} the point \(a\), so it may depend on \(a\); in
Equation~\ref{eq-uniform-continuity}, one \(\delta\) must work
\emph{simultaneously for all points} of \([a,b]\). For instance,
\(f(x)=\tfrac1x\) is continuous on \((0,1]\), but near \(0\) it becomes
steeper and steeper, and no single \(\delta\) works for a given
\(\varepsilon\). On a \textbf{closed, bounded} interval this cannot
happen: a continuous \(f\colon[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}\) is automatically
uniformly continuous. Like the Extreme Value Theorem, this can be proved
with a bisection / nested interval argument in the spirit of the proof
of Theorem~\ref{thm-ivt}.

\label{rem-darboux-informal}

\end{refremark}

\begin{refremark}
Continuity really matters. For the Dirichlet function of
Example~\ref{exm-dirichlet-discontinuous} on \([0,1]\), every segment of
every partition contains both rational and irrational points. Choosing
all sample points rational gives
\(RS(P,S) = \sum_i 1\cdot\Delta_i = 1\); choosing them all irrational
gives \(RS(P,S)=0\) --- for \emph{every} partition, however fine. So the
Riemann sums have no common limit, and the Dirichlet function is not
Riemann integrable.

\label{rem-dirichlet-not-integrable}

\end{refremark}

\section{Verifying the Riemann
Integral}\label{verifying-the-riemann-integral}

Before computing anything new, let us check that
Definition~\ref{def-riemann-integral} gives the right answer for areas
we already know from geometry. By
Theorem~\ref{thm-riemann-equipartition} we may use whichever of the
three sums is most convenient. Throughout, \(\Delta = \frac{b-a}{n}\)
and \(x_i = a + i\Delta\).

\begin{example}[]\protect\hypertarget{exm-integral-constant}{}\label{exm-integral-constant}

\hfill\break
Let \(f(x) = c\). Every sample point gives height \(c\), so \[
LHS_n = RHS_n = \sum_{i=1}^n \frac{b-a}{n}\cdot c = n\cdot\frac{b-a}{n}\cdot c = (b-a)\,c,
\] and hence \[
\int_a^b c\,dx = \lim_{n\to\infty} LHS_n = \lim_{n\to\infty}(b-a)\,c = (b-a)\,c,
\] the area of a rectangle with base \(b-a\) and height \(c\). ✓

\end{example}

\begin{example}[]\protect\hypertarget{exm-integral-x}{}\label{exm-integral-x}

\hfill\break
Let \(f(x) = x\). Using right endpoints \(x_i = a+\frac{b-a}{n}\,i\), \[
\begin{aligned}
RHS_n &= \sum_{i=1}^n \frac{b-a}{n}\cdot f\Big(a+\frac{b-a}{n}\,i\Big)
= \frac{b-a}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n \Big(a+\frac{b-a}{n}\,i\Big) \\
&= \frac{b-a}{n}\Big[\Big(a+\frac{b-a}{n}\cdot1\Big)+\Big(a+\frac{b-a}{n}\cdot2\Big)
+\cdots+\Big(a+\frac{b-a}{n}\cdot n\Big)\Big] \\
&= \frac{b-a}{n}\Big(n\,a + \frac{b-a}{n}\cdot\frac{n(n+1)}{2}\Big) \\
&= (b-a)\,a + \frac{b-a}{n}\cdot\frac{b-a}{n}\cdot\frac{n(n+1)}{2} \\
&= (b-a)\,a + \frac{(b-a)^2}{2}\cdot\frac{n+1}{n}
\;\xrightarrow{\;n\to\infty\;}\; (b-a)\,a + \frac{(b-a)^2}{2}.
\end{aligned}
\] Here we used \(1+2+\cdots+n = \frac{n(n+1)}{2}\) and
\(\frac{n+1}{n}\to1\).

For \(0\le a<b\) this is exactly what geometry predicts: the region
under \(y=x\) between \(a\) and \(b\) is a rectangle of base \(b-a\) and
height \(a\) (area \((b-a)\,a\)), topped by a right triangle with both
legs of length \(b-a\) (area \(\frac{(b-a)^2}{2}\)). ✓ Simplifying, \[
\int_a^b x\,dx = (b-a)\Big(a + \frac{b-a}{2}\Big) = \frac{(b-a)(b+a)}{2} = \frac{b^2-a^2}{2}.
\]

\end{example}

\begin{exercise}[]\protect\hypertarget{exr-integral-linear}{}\label{exr-integral-linear}

Verify this for an arbitrary linear function \(f(x) = mx + c\): compute
\(\lim_{n\to\infty}RHS_n\) and check that the result agrees with the
area of the trapezoid under the graph (when \(f\ge0\) on \([a,b]\)).

\end{exercise}

\section{\texorpdfstring{``New'' Functions:
\(x^2\)}{``New'' Functions: x\^{}2}}\label{new-functions-x2}

The examples above only reproduced areas we already knew. The first
genuinely new area is the one under a parabola. Let \(f(x) = x^2\). Then
\[
RHS_n = \frac{b-a}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n f\Big(a+\frac{b-a}{n}\,i\Big)
= \frac{b-a}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n\Big(a+\frac{b-a}{n}\,i\Big)^2.
\]

To make life a little easier, take \(a=0\): \[
\begin{aligned}
RHS_n &= \frac{b}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n\Big(\frac{b}{n}\,i\Big)^2
= \frac{b}{n}\cdot\frac{b^2}{n^2}\,\big(1^2+2^2+\cdots+n^2\big) \\
&= \frac{b^3}{n^3}\cdot\frac{n(n+1)(2n+1)}{6}
= \frac{b^3}{6}\Big(1+\frac1n\Big)\Big(2+\frac1n\Big)
\;\xrightarrow{\;n\to\infty\;}\; \frac{b^3\cdot 2}{6} = \frac{b^3}{3}.
\end{aligned}
\] So \[
\int_0^b x^2\,dx = \frac{b^3}{3}.
\]

\begin{exercise}[]\protect\hypertarget{exr-sum-of-squares}{}\label{exr-sum-of-squares}

Prove by induction that \(1^2+2^2+\cdots+n^2 = \dfrac{n(n+1)(2n+1)}{6}\)
for all \(n\in\mathbb{N}\).

\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}[]\protect\hypertarget{exr-integral-x-squared-general}{}\label{exr-integral-x-squared-general}

Generalize the computation above to show that for arbitrary \(a<b\), \[
\int_a^b x^2\,dx = \frac{b^3-a^3}{3}.
\] (Expand \(\big(a+\frac{b-a}{n}\,i\big)^2\) and use the formulas for
\(\sum i\) and \(\sum i^2\).)

\end{exercise}

\section{Summary}\label{summary-7}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  The area of the unit circle is the limit of the areas
  \(A_n = \frac n2\sin\!\big(\frac{2\pi}{n}\big)\) of inscribed regular
  \(n\)-gons; the sequence is increasing and bounded, so it converges by

  \begin{enumerate}
  \def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
  \setcounter{enumi}{1}
  \tightlist
  \item
  \end{enumerate}
\item
  For areas under a graph we use \textbf{rectangles}: a
  \textbf{partition} \(P\) of \([a,b]\) and \textbf{sample points} \(S\)
  give the \textbf{Riemann sum}
  \(RS(P,S) = \sum_{i=1}^n f(x_i^*)\,\Delta_i\)
  (Definition~\ref{def-riemann-sum}). We improve the approximation by
  refining the partition so that the \textbf{mesh}
  \(\|P\|=\max_i\Delta_i\) tends to \(0\).
\item
  Restricting to \textbf{equipartitions} with left endpoints, right
  endpoints, or midpoints gives three sequences \(LHS_n\), \(RHS_n\),
  \(MS_n\). For continuous \(f\) they have a common limit
  (Theorem~\ref{thm-riemann-equipartition}), which \emph{defines}
  \(\int_a^b f(x)\,dx\) (Definition~\ref{def-riemann-integral}).
\item
  More generally, for continuous \(f\) \textbf{every} sequence of
  Riemann sums with mesh \(\to0\) converges to the same number
  (Theorem~\ref{thm-riemann-general}). The idea: every Riemann sum is
  trapped between the \textbf{lower and upper sums}
  \(L(P)\le RS(P,S)\le U(P)\); refining pushes these toward each other;
  and \textbf{uniform continuity} makes the gap \(U(P)-L(P)\) smaller
  than \(\varepsilon(b-a)\) once the mesh is small
  (Remark~\ref{rem-darboux-informal}). Without continuity this can fail,
  as the Dirichlet function shows
  (Remark~\ref{rem-dirichlet-not-integrable}).
\item
  The definition reproduces known areas --- \(\int_a^b c\,dx = (b-a)c\)
  (Example~\ref{exm-integral-constant}) and
  \(\int_a^b x\,dx = \frac{b^2-a^2}{2}\) (Example~\ref{exm-integral-x})
  --- and computes new ones: \(\int_0^b x^2\,dx = \frac{b^3}{3}\).
\end{itemize}

notebook lets you build those rectangles, look at them, and watch the
Riemann sums approach the integral as the partition gets finer.

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ \_to\_exact(v):}
    \CommentTok{"""Turn a number into an exact sympy number (0.25 {-}\textgreater{} 1/4, pi stays pi)."""}
\NormalTok{    v }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ sp.sympify(v)}
    \ControlFlowTok{if} \BuiltInTok{isinstance}\NormalTok{(v, sp.Float):}
\NormalTok{        v }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ sp.nsimplify(v, rational}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ v}


\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ \_fmt(v, digits}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{):}
    \CommentTok{"""Format a float compactly, e.g. 0.0625, 1.41421, {-}3.2e{-}05."""}
\NormalTok{    s }\OperatorTok{=} \SpecialStringTok{f\textquotesingle{}}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{(v)}\SpecialCharTok{:.}\NormalTok{\{digits\}g}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{\textquotesingle{}}
    \ControlFlowTok{if} \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}e\textquotesingle{}} \KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ s:}
\NormalTok{        mant, ex }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ s.split(}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}e\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{        s }\OperatorTok{=} \VerbatimStringTok{rf\textquotesingle{}}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{mant}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\VerbatimStringTok{ }\ErrorTok{\textbackslash{}}\VerbatimStringTok{cdot 10}\DecValTok{\^{}}\CharTok{\{\{}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{(ex)}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\CharTok{\}\}}\VerbatimStringTok{\textquotesingle{}}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ s}


\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ \_join\_terms(terms, n, max\_terms):}
    \CommentTok{"""Join LaTeX terms with + and {-} signs; if n is large, abbreviate with + ... +."""}
    \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ n }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ max\_terms:}
\NormalTok{        terms }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ terms[:}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ [}\VerbatimStringTok{r\textquotesingle{}}\ErrorTok{\textbackslash{}}\VerbatimStringTok{cdots\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ terms[}\OperatorTok{{-}}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{:]}
\NormalTok{    out }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ terms[}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{]}
    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ t }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ terms[}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{:]:}
        \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ t.startswith(}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}{-}\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{):}
\NormalTok{            out }\OperatorTok{+=} \StringTok{\textquotesingle{} {-} \textquotesingle{}} \OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ t[}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{:].lstrip()}
        \ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{:}
\NormalTok{            out }\OperatorTok{+=} \StringTok{\textquotesingle{} + \textquotesingle{}} \OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ t}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ out}


\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ riemann\_sum(f, a, b, n, method}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}left\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{, x}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{, show}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{                show\_integral}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{False}\NormalTok{, max\_terms}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{):}
    \CommentTok{"""}
\CommentTok{    Compute (and by default draw) a Riemann sum for f on [a, b].}

\CommentTok{    Parameters}
\CommentTok{    {-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}}
\CommentTok{    f : sympy expression in one variable, e.g. x**2 or sp.sin(x)}
\CommentTok{    a, b : the integration bounds, a \textless{} b (numbers or sympy numbers like sp.pi)}
\CommentTok{    n : number of subintervals of equal length}
\CommentTok{    method : \textquotesingle{}left\textquotesingle{}, \textquotesingle{}right\textquotesingle{}, or \textquotesingle{}midpoint\textquotesingle{}}
\CommentTok{    x : the variable of f (only needed if f has more than one symbol)}
\CommentTok{    show : if False, just return the value (no picture, no computation lines)}
\CommentTok{    show\_integral : also display the exact integral and the error of the sum}
\CommentTok{    max\_terms : largest n for which every term of the sum is written out;}
\CommentTok{                for larger n the sum is abbreviated with "+ ... +"}

\CommentTok{    Returns}
\CommentTok{    {-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}}
\CommentTok{    The value of the Riemann sum as a float.}
\CommentTok{    """}
    \CommentTok{\# {-}{-}{-}{-} check and prepare the input {-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}}
\NormalTok{    method }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ \_METHODS.get(}\BuiltInTok{str}\NormalTok{(method).lower().replace(}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{} \textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{))}
    \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ method }\KeywordTok{is} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
        \ControlFlowTok{raise} \PreprocessorTok{ValueError}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"method must be \textquotesingle{}left\textquotesingle{}, \textquotesingle{}right\textquotesingle{}, or \textquotesingle{}midpoint\textquotesingle{}"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    n }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{(n)}
    \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ n }\OperatorTok{\textless{}} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{:}
        \ControlFlowTok{raise} \PreprocessorTok{ValueError}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}n must be a positive integer\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    f }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ sp.sympify(f)}
    \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ x }\KeywordTok{is} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
\NormalTok{        syms }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{sorted}\NormalTok{(f.free\_symbols, key}\OperatorTok{=}\BuiltInTok{str}\NormalTok{)}
        \ControlFlowTok{if} \BuiltInTok{len}\NormalTok{(syms) }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{:}
            \ControlFlowTok{raise} \PreprocessorTok{ValueError}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f\textquotesingle{}f has several symbols }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{syms}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{; pass the variable as x=...\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{        x }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ syms[}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{] }\ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ syms }\ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{ sp.Symbol(}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}x\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    a, b }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ \_to\_exact(a), \_to\_exact(b)}
    \ControlFlowTok{if} \KeywordTok{not}\NormalTok{ (sp.N(a) }\OperatorTok{\textless{}}\NormalTok{ sp.N(b)):}
        \ControlFlowTok{raise} \PreprocessorTok{ValueError}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}need a \textless{} b\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}

    \CommentTok{\# {-}{-}{-}{-} the partition and the sample points {-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}}
\NormalTok{    delta }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (b }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ a) }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ n                    }\CommentTok{\# exact length of each subinterval}
\NormalTok{    shift }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ \{}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}left\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}right\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}midpoint\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: sp.Rational(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)\}[method]}
\NormalTok{    offset }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ \{}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}left\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}right\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}midpoint\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: sp.Rational(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)\}[method]}

\NormalTok{    f\_num }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ sp.lambdify(x, f, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}numpy\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    a\_f, b\_f, d\_f }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{(a), }\BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{(b), }\BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{(delta)}
\NormalTok{    left\_edges }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ a\_f }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ d\_f }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ np.arange(n)}
\NormalTok{    samples }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ left\_edges }\OperatorTok{+} \BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{(shift) }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ d\_f}
    \ControlFlowTok{with}\NormalTok{ np.errstate(}\BuiltInTok{all}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}ignore\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{):}
\NormalTok{        heights }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.broadcast\_to(np.asarray(f\_num(samples), dtype}\OperatorTok{=}\BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{), samples.shape)}
    \ControlFlowTok{if} \KeywordTok{not}\NormalTok{ np.}\BuiltInTok{all}\NormalTok{(np.isfinite(heights)):}
\NormalTok{        bad }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ samples[}\OperatorTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{np.isfinite(heights)][}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{]}
        \ControlFlowTok{raise} \PreprocessorTok{ValueError}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f\textquotesingle{}f is not defined (or infinite) at the sample point }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{bad}\SpecialCharTok{:g\}}\SpecialStringTok{; \textquotesingle{}}
                         \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}try a different method or interval\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    value }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{(np.}\BuiltInTok{sum}\NormalTok{(heights) }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ d\_f)}
    \ControlFlowTok{if} \BuiltInTok{abs}\NormalTok{(value) }\OperatorTok{\textless{}} \FloatTok{1e{-}12} \OperatorTok{*} \BuiltInTok{max}\NormalTok{(}\FloatTok{1.0}\NormalTok{, }\BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{(np.}\BuiltInTok{sum}\NormalTok{(np.}\BuiltInTok{abs}\NormalTok{(heights)) }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ d\_f)):}
\NormalTok{        value }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{0.0}          \CommentTok{\# the exact answer is 0; the rest is rounding noise}

    \ControlFlowTok{if} \KeywordTok{not}\NormalTok{ show:}
        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ value}

    \CommentTok{\# {-}{-}{-}{-} the picture {-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}}
\NormalTok{    fig, ax }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ plt.subplots()}
\NormalTok{    colors }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.where(heights }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}=} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}tab:blue\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}tab:red\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    edge }\OperatorTok{=} \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}black\textquotesingle{}} \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ n }\OperatorTok{\textless{}=} \DecValTok{100} \ControlFlowTok{else} \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}none\textquotesingle{}}
\NormalTok{    ax.bar(left\_edges, heights, width}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{d\_f, align}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}edge\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{, color}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{colors,}
\NormalTok{           alpha}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{0.35}\NormalTok{, edgecolor}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{edge, linewidth}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{0.8}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    xs }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.linspace(a\_f, b\_f, }\DecValTok{1000}\NormalTok{)}
    \ControlFlowTok{with}\NormalTok{ np.errstate(}\BuiltInTok{all}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}ignore\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{):}
\NormalTok{        ys }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.array(np.broadcast\_to(np.asarray(f\_num(xs), dtype}\OperatorTok{=}\BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{), xs.shape))}
\NormalTok{    ys[}\OperatorTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{np.isfinite(ys)] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.nan}
\NormalTok{    ax.plot(xs, ys, color}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}black\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{, linewidth}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, label}\OperatorTok{=}\SpecialStringTok{f\textquotesingle{}$f(}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{x}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{) = }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{sp}\SpecialCharTok{.}\NormalTok{latex(f)}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{$\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
    \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ n }\OperatorTok{\textless{}=} \DecValTok{60}\NormalTok{:}
\NormalTok{        ax.plot(samples, heights, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}o\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{, color}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}darkorange\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{, markersize}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{                label}\OperatorTok{=}\VerbatimStringTok{r\textquotesingle{}}\DecValTok{$}\KeywordTok{(}\VerbatimStringTok{x\_i}\DecValTok{\^{}}\OperatorTok{*}\VerbatimStringTok{, f}\KeywordTok{(}\VerbatimStringTok{x\_i}\DecValTok{\^{}}\OperatorTok{*}\KeywordTok{))}\DecValTok{$}\VerbatimStringTok{\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    ax.axhline(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, color}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}gray\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{, linewidth}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{0.8}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    pad }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{0.05} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ (b\_f }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ a\_f)}
\NormalTok{    ax.set\_xlim(a\_f }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ pad, b\_f }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ pad)}
    \CommentTok{\# if f blows up somewhere, keep the rectangles in view instead of the spike}
\NormalTok{    h\_lo, h\_hi }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{min}\NormalTok{(}\FloatTok{0.0}\NormalTok{, heights.}\BuiltInTok{min}\NormalTok{()), }\BuiltInTok{max}\NormalTok{(}\FloatTok{0.0}\NormalTok{, heights.}\BuiltInTok{max}\NormalTok{())}
\NormalTok{    span }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ h\_hi }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ h\_lo}
\NormalTok{    y\_lo, y\_hi }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.nanmin(ys), np.nanmax(ys)}
    \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ span }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}} \DecValTok{0} \KeywordTok{and}\NormalTok{ (y\_hi }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ h\_hi }\OperatorTok{+} \DecValTok{2} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ span }\KeywordTok{or}\NormalTok{ y\_lo }\OperatorTok{\textless{}}\NormalTok{ h\_lo }\OperatorTok{{-}} \DecValTok{2} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ span):}
\NormalTok{        lo, hi }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{max}\NormalTok{(y\_lo, h\_lo }\OperatorTok{{-}} \FloatTok{0.5} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ span), }\BuiltInTok{min}\NormalTok{(y\_hi, h\_hi }\OperatorTok{+} \FloatTok{0.5} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ span)}
\NormalTok{        ax.set\_ylim(}\BuiltInTok{min}\NormalTok{(lo, h\_lo) }\OperatorTok{{-}} \FloatTok{0.05} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ span, }\BuiltInTok{max}\NormalTok{(hi, h\_hi) }\OperatorTok{+} \FloatTok{0.05} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ span)}
\NormalTok{    ax.set\_xlabel(}\SpecialStringTok{f\textquotesingle{}$}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{x}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{$\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    ax.set\_title(}\SpecialStringTok{f\textquotesingle{}}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{method}\SpecialCharTok{.}\NormalTok{capitalize()}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{ Riemann sum with n = }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{n}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    handles, labels }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ ax.get\_legend\_handles\_labels()}
    \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ np.}\BuiltInTok{any}\NormalTok{(heights }\OperatorTok{\textless{}} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{):}
\NormalTok{        handles }\OperatorTok{+=}\NormalTok{ [Patch(color}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}tab:blue\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{, alpha}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{0.35}\NormalTok{), Patch(color}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}tab:red\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{, alpha}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{0.35}\NormalTok{)]}
\NormalTok{        labels }\OperatorTok{+=}\NormalTok{ [}\VerbatimStringTok{r\textquotesingle{}}\DecValTok{$}\VerbatimStringTok{f}\KeywordTok{(}\VerbatimStringTok{x\_i}\DecValTok{\^{}}\OperatorTok{*}\KeywordTok{)}\VerbatimStringTok{ }\ErrorTok{\textbackslash{}}\VerbatimStringTok{geq 0}\DecValTok{$}\VerbatimStringTok{\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{, }\VerbatimStringTok{r\textquotesingle{}}\DecValTok{$}\VerbatimStringTok{f}\KeywordTok{(}\VerbatimStringTok{x\_i}\DecValTok{\^{}}\OperatorTok{*}\KeywordTok{)}\VerbatimStringTok{ \textless{} 0}\DecValTok{$}\VerbatimStringTok{\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{]}
\NormalTok{    ax.legend(handles, labels, loc}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}best\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    ax.grid(}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{, alpha}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{0.3}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    plt.show()}

    \CommentTok{\# {-}{-}{-}{-} the computation, line by line {-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}}
\NormalTok{    A, B, D }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ sp.latex(a), sp.latex(b), sp.latex(delta)}
\NormalTok{    fx }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ sp.latex(f)}

        \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}left\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{:     }\VerbatimStringTok{r\textquotesingle{}x\_i}\DecValTok{\^{}}\OperatorTok{*}\VerbatimStringTok{ = x\_\{i{-}1\} = a }\OperatorTok{+}\VerbatimStringTok{ }\KeywordTok{(}\VerbatimStringTok{i{-}1}\KeywordTok{)}\DecValTok{\textbackslash{}D}\VerbatimStringTok{elta\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{,}

\NormalTok{    display(Math(}\VerbatimStringTok{rf\textquotesingle{}}\DecValTok{\textbackslash{}D}\VerbatimStringTok{elta = }\CharTok{\textbackslash{}f}\VerbatimStringTok{rac}\CharTok{\{\{}\VerbatimStringTok{b{-}a}\CharTok{\}\}\{\{}\VerbatimStringTok{n}\CharTok{\}\}}\VerbatimStringTok{ = }\CharTok{\textbackslash{}f}\VerbatimStringTok{rac}\CharTok{\{\{}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{B}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\VerbatimStringTok{ {-} }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{a\_paren}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\CharTok{\}\}\{\{}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{n}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\CharTok{\}\}}\VerbatimStringTok{ = }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{D}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\VerbatimStringTok{\textquotesingle{}}
                 \OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ (}\VerbatimStringTok{rf\textquotesingle{} }\CharTok{\textbackslash{}a}\VerbatimStringTok{pprox }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{\_fmt(d\_f)}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\VerbatimStringTok{\textquotesingle{}} \ControlFlowTok{if} \KeywordTok{not}\NormalTok{ delta.is\_Rational }\ControlFlowTok{else} \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
