f'(x) = \frac{m(x+h)+b-mx-b}{h} \qquad\text{(wrong)}
\] is false: the left side is a number that does not depend on \(h\),
the right side is a function of \(h\) (and undefined at \(h=0\)). On the
other hand, the step \(\frac{mh}{h}=m\) inside the limit is legitimate,
because the limit only involves \(h\neq0\)
(Remark~\ref{rem-derivative-limit-definition}).

\label{rem-writing-limits}

\end{refremark}

\subsection{Power functions with integer
exponents}\label{power-functions-with-integer-exponents}

We need an algebraic identity that generalizes
\(x_1^2-x^2 = (x_1-x)(x_1+x)\) and
\(x_1^3-x^3=(x_1-x)(x_1^2+x_1x+x^2)\), which we used in
Example~\ref{exm-derivative-square} and
Example~\ref{exm-derivative-cube}. For every integer \(n\ge1\),
\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-power-difference}{
x_1^n - x^n = (x_1-x)\big(x_1^{n-1} + x_1^{n-2}x + \cdots + x_1x^{n-2} + x^{n-1}\big).
}\end{equation} To check it, multiply out the right-hand side: \(x_1\)
times the bracket gives \(x_1^n + x_1^{n-1}x + \cdots + x_1x^{n-1}\),
and \(-x\) times the bracket gives
\(-x_1^{n-1}x - \cdots - x_1x^{n-1} - x^n\). All terms cancel except
\(x_1^n\) and \(-x^n\).

\begin{proposition}[Power rule, positive
integers]\protect\hypertarget{prp-power-rule-positive}{}\label{prp-power-rule-positive}

Let \(n\ge 1\) be an integer and \(f(x)=x^n\). Then \(f\) is
differentiable at every \(x\in\mathbb{R}\), and \[
f'(x) = n\,x^{n-1}.
\]

\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For \(x_1\neq x\), Equation~\ref{eq-power-difference} gives \[
\frac{f(x_1)-f(x)}{x_1-x} = \frac{x_1^n-x^n}{x_1-x}
= x_1^{n-1} + x_1^{n-2}x + \cdots + x_1x^{n-2} + x^{n-1}.
\] The right-hand side is a polynomial in \(x_1\), hence continuous, so
we can take the limit \(x_1\to x\) by plugging in \(x_1=x\)
(Proposition~\ref{prp-continuity-via-limit}). Each of the \(n\) terms
becomes \(x^{n-1}\), so \[
f'(x) = \lim_{x_1\to x}\big(x_1^{n-1} + x_1^{n-2}x + \cdots + x_1x^{n-2} + x^{n-1}\big) = n\,x^{n-1}.
\]
\end{proof}

For negative exponents we must assume \(x\neq 0\), since \(x^{-n}\) is
not defined at \(0\) (Definition~\ref{def-power-function}).

\begin{proposition}[Power rule, negative
integers]\protect\hypertarget{prp-power-rule-negative}{}\label{prp-power-rule-negative}

Let \(n\ge1\) be an integer and \(f(x)=x^{-n}\). Then \(f\) is
differentiable at every \(x\neq0\), and \[
f'(x) = -n\,x^{-n-1}.
\] In other words, with \(\beta=-n\):
\(\;(x^\beta)' = \beta\,x^{\beta-1}\), the same formula as for positive
exponents.

\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Let \(x\neq 0\), and let \(x_1\neq x\) with \(x_1\neq 0\). Then \[
\frac{f(x_1)-f(x)}{x_1-x} = \frac{\dfrac{1}{x_1^n}-\dfrac{1}{x^n}}{x_1-x}
= \frac{x^n-x_1^n}{x_1^n x^n\,(x_1-x)}
= -\frac{1}{x_1^nx^n}\cdot\frac{x_1^n-x^n}{x_1-x}.
\] By Equation~\ref{eq-power-difference}, \[
\frac{f(x_1)-f(x)}{x_1-x}
= -\frac{x_1^{n-1} + x_1^{n-2}x + \cdots + x_1x^{n-2} + x^{n-1}}{x_1^n x^n}.
\] As \(x_1\to x\), the numerator tends to \(n x^{n-1}\) (as in the
previous proof) and the denominator to \(x^{2n}\neq0\). By the limit
laws (Proposition~\ref{prp-function-limit-laws}), \[
f'(x) = -\frac{n\,x^{n-1}}{x^{2n}} = -n\,x^{-n-1}.
\]
\end{proof}

So the \textbf{power rule} \[
\frac{d}{dx}\,x^\alpha = \alpha\, x^{\alpha-1}
\] holds for every integer \(\alpha\) (for \(\alpha=0\) both sides are
\(0\), if \(x\neq0\)). We will extend it to arbitrary real exponents
\(\alpha\) later. We have already seen one instance:
\((\sqrt x)' = \frac{1}{2\sqrt x} = \frac12 x^{-1/2}\)
(Example~\ref{exm-derivative-sqrt}).

\subsection{Trigonometric functions}\label{trigonometric-functions-1}

To differentiate \(\sin\) and \(\cos\) we need two limits. Here, as
always in calculus, angles are measured in \textbf{radians}.

\begin{lemma}[]\protect\hypertarget{lem-trig-limits}{}\label{lem-trig-limits}

\hfill\break
\[
\lim_{h\to0}\frac{\sin h}{h} = 1, \qquad\qquad \lim_{h\to0}\frac{\cos h - 1}{h} = 0.
\]

\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let \(0<h<\frac\pi2\), and consider the unit circle with \(O=(0,0)\),
\(A=(1,0)\), \(P=(\cos h,\sin h)\) and \(T=(1,\tan h)\)
(Figure~\ref{fig-sin-limit}). The triangle \(OAP\) lies inside the
circular sector \(OAP\), which lies inside the triangle \(OAT\).
Comparing areas, \[
\frac12\sin h \;<\; \frac h2 \;<\; \frac12\tan h .
\] (The sector is the fraction \(\frac{h}{2\pi}\) of the disk, whose
area is \(\pi\), so its area is \(\frac h2\). This is where radian
measure enters.) Dividing by \(\frac12\sin h>0\) gives
\(1<\frac{h}{\sin h}<\frac{1}{\cos h}\), that is, \[
\cos h \;<\; \frac{\sin h}{h} \;<\; 1 .
\] Since \(\cos(-h)=\cos h\) and
\(\frac{\sin(-h)}{-h}=\frac{\sin h}{h}\), these inequalities hold for
\(0<\lvert h \rvert<\frac\pi2\). Now let \(h_n\to0\) with \(h_n\neq0\).
Eventually \(\lvert h_n \rvert<\frac\pi2\), and
\(\cos h_n\to\cos 0 = 1\) by continuity of \(\cos\)
(Remark~\ref{rem-elementary-continuous}). By the squeeze theorem
(Proposition~\ref{prp-squeeze}), \(\frac{\sin h_n}{h_n}\to1\). Since
this holds for every such sequence, the first limit follows
(Definition~\ref{def-function-limit}).

For the second limit, multiply numerator and denominator by \(\cos h+1\)
and use \(\cos^2h-1 = -\sin^2h\): \[
\frac{\cos h-1}{h} = \frac{\cos^2 h-1}{h\,(\cos h+1)} = -\frac{\sin h}{h}\cdot\frac{\sin h}{\cos h+1}
\;\longrightarrow\; -1\cdot\frac{0}{2} = 0
\] by the first limit, continuity of \(\sin\) and \(\cos\), and the
limit laws (Proposition~\ref{prp-function-limit-laws}).
\end{proof}

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{13_differentiation_rules_files/figure-pdf/fig-sin-limit-output-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-sin-limit}For \(0<h<\pi/2\): triangle \(OAP\)
\(\subset\) sector \(OAP\) \(\subset\) triangle \(OAT\), so
\(\tfrac12\sin h<\tfrac h2<\tfrac12\tan h\).}

\end{figure}%

\begin{proposition}[Derivatives of sine and
cosine]\protect\hypertarget{prp-derivative-sin-cos}{}\label{prp-derivative-sin-cos}

For all \(x\in\mathbb{R}\), \[
(\sin x)' = \cos x, \qquad\qquad (\cos x)' = -\sin x .
\]

\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
By the addition formula \(\sin(x+y) = \sin x\cos y + \cos x\sin y\), for
\(h\neq0\), \[
\begin{aligned}
\frac{\sin(x+h)-\sin x}{h}
&= \frac{\sin x\cos h + \cos x\sin h - \sin x}{h} \\
&= \sin x\cdot\frac{\cos h-1}{h} + \cos x\cdot\frac{\sin h}{h}.
\end{aligned}
\] Here \(x\) is fixed, so \(\sin x\) and \(\cos x\) are constants. By
Lemma~\ref{lem-trig-limits} and the limit laws
(Proposition~\ref{prp-function-limit-laws}), \[
\lim_{h\to0}\frac{\sin(x+h)-\sin x}{h} = \sin x\cdot 0 + \cos x\cdot 1 = \cos x .
\] The derivative of \(\cos\) is Exercise~\ref{exr-derivative-cos}.
\end{proof}

\begin{exercise}[]\protect\hypertarget{exr-derivative-cos}{}\label{exr-derivative-cos}

Use the addition formula \(\cos(x+y) = \cos x\cos y - \sin x\sin y\) and
Lemma~\ref{lem-trig-limits} to show that \((\cos x)' = -\sin x\).

\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
For \(h\neq0\),
\begin{align}
\frac{\cos(x+h)-\cos x}{h} & = \frac{\cos x\cos h - \sin x\sin h-\cos x}{h} \\
& = \cos x\cdot\frac{\cos h-1}{h} - \sin x\cdot\frac{\sin h}{h}
\;\longrightarrow\; \cos x\cdot 0 - \sin x\cdot 1 = -\sin x .
\end{align}

\end{solution}

\section{Sum and Constant Multiple
Rules}\label{sum-and-constant-multiple-rules}

As for the integral (Proposition~\ref{prp-sum-rule},
Proposition~\ref{prp-constant-multiple}), there are basic rules that
follow directly from the definition and the limit laws.

\begin{proposition}[Sum
rule]\protect\hypertarget{prp-derivative-sum-rule}{}\label{prp-derivative-sum-rule}

If \(f'(x)\) and \(g'(x)\) exist and \(\varphi(x) = f(x)+g(x)\), then
\(\varphi'(x)\) exists and \[
\varphi'(x) = f'(x)+g'(x).
\]

\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For \(h\neq0\), \[
\frac{\varphi(x+h)-\varphi(x)}{h} = \frac{f(x+h)+g(x+h)-f(x)-g(x)}{h}
= \frac{f(x+h)-f(x)}{h} + \frac{g(x+h)-g(x)}{h}.
\] As \(h\to0\), the first quotient on the right tends to \(f'(x)\) and
the second to \(g'(x)\). By the limit law for sums
(Proposition~\ref{prp-function-limit-laws}), the left side tends to
\(f'(x)+g'(x)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Constant multiple
rule]\protect\hypertarget{prp-derivative-constant-multiple}{}\label{prp-derivative-constant-multiple}

If \(f'(x)\) exists, \(c\in\mathbb{R}\), and \(\psi(x) = c\,f(x)\), then
\(\psi'(x)\) exists and \[
\psi'(x) = c\,f'(x).
\]

\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For \(h\neq0\), \[
\frac{\psi(x+h)-\psi(x)}{h} = c\cdot\frac{f(x+h)-f(x)}{h} \;\longrightarrow\; c\,f'(x)
\qquad(h\to 0),
\] by the limit law for products, applied to the constant function
\(c\).
\end{proof}

Combining the two rules, \((\alpha f+\beta g)' = \alpha f'+\beta g'\)
for all constants \(\alpha,\beta\); in particular \((f-g)' = f'-g'\).
Together with the power rule, this lets us differentiate every
polynomial.

\begin{example}[]\protect\hypertarget{exm-polynomial-derivative}{}\label{exm-polynomial-derivative}

\hfill\break
For a polynomial
\(p(x) = a_nx^n + a_{n-1}x^{n-1} + \cdots + a_1x + a_0\),
Proposition~\ref{prp-power-rule-positive} and the two rules give \[
p'(x) = n a_n x^{n-1} + (n-1)a_{n-1}x^{n-2} + \cdots + a_1 .
\] For example, \((x^3-3x)' = 3x^2-3\). This is negative for
\(\lvert x \rvert<1\) and positive for \(\lvert x \rvert>1\), so by
Proposition~\ref{prp-monotone-derivative}, \(x^3-3x\) is strictly
increasing on \((-\infty,-1)\), strictly decreasing on \((-1,1)\), and
strictly increasing on \((1,\infty)\), with critical points \(\pm1\)
(Figure~\ref{fig-shape-from-derivative}). Similarly, \[
\big(5x^{-2} + 2\sin x - 4\cos x\big)' = -10x^{-3} + 2\cos x + 4\sin x \qquad (x\neq0).
\]

\end{example}

\begin{refremark}
As for integrals (Remark~\ref{rem-no-product-rule}), there is
\textbf{no} rule of the form \((fg)' = f'g'\). For \(f(x)=g(x)=x\), we
have \((x\cdot x)' = (x^2)' = 2x\), but \(f'(x)\,g'(x) = 1\cdot1=1\).
The correct \emph{product rule} will come next, together with rules for
quotients and compositions.

\label{rem-no-product-rule-derivative}

\end{refremark}

\section{Differentiability
vs.~Continuity}\label{differentiability-vs.-continuity}

\begin{theorem}[]\protect\hypertarget{thm-differentiable-continuous}{}\label{thm-differentiable-continuous}

\hfill\break
If \(f\) is differentiable at \(x\), then \(f\) is continuous at \(x\).

\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Assume \[
\lim_{h\to0}\frac{f(x+h)-f(x)}{h} = f'(x), \qquad -\infty < f'(x) < \infty .
\] For \(h\neq 0\) we can write
\(f(x+h)-f(x) = \dfrac{f(x+h)-f(x)}{h}\cdot h\). By the limit law for
products (Proposition~\ref{prp-function-limit-laws}), \[
\lim_{h\to0}\big(f(x+h)-f(x)\big)
= \lim_{h\to0}\frac{f(x+h)-f(x)}{h}\cdot\lim_{h\to0} h = f'(x)\cdot 0 = 0 .
\] Hence \(f(x+h)\to f(x)\) as \(h\to0\). Substituting \(x_1=x+h\) as in
the previous chapter, this says \(\lim_{x_1\to x}f(x_1) = f(x)\), so
\(f\) is continuous at \(x\)
(Proposition~\ref{prp-continuity-via-limit}).
\end{proof}

\begin{refremark}
The proof uses that \(f'(x)\) is a (finite) real number: the limit law
gives ``\(f'(x)\cdot 0 = 0\)'', and ``\(\infty\cdot0\)'' has no meaning.
This is not a technicality. For the sign function,
\(\operatorname{sgn}(x) = 1\) for \(x>0\), \(\operatorname{sgn}(0)=0\),
\(\operatorname{sgn}(x)=-1\) for \(x<0\), the difference quotient at
\(0\) is \[
\frac{\operatorname{sgn}(h)-\operatorname{sgn}(0)}{h} = \frac{1}{\lvert h \rvert} \longrightarrow \infty
\qquad (h\to 0),
\] yet \(\operatorname{sgn}\) is not continuous at \(0\).

\label{rem-finite-derivative}

\end{refremark}

As a consequence, every function we have differentiated so far
(polynomials, \(x^{-n}\), \(\sqrt x\), \(\sin\), \(\cos\)) is continuous
wherever it is differentiable, without a separate continuity proof
(compare Remark~\ref{rem-elementary-continuous}).

\textbf{The converse is false.} There are functions that are continuous
everywhere but not differentiable at some point. The simplest is
\(f(x)=\lvert x \rvert\) at \(x=0\) (Example~\ref{exm-abs-no-tangent}).
For \(h\neq 0\), \[
\frac{f(0+h)-f(0)}{h} = \frac{\lvert h \rvert}{h} = \begin{cases} 1 & h>0, \\ -1 & h<0. \end{cases}
\] The one-sided limits (Definition~\ref{def-one-sided-limits}) exist
but disagree: the \textbf{right-hand derivative} at \(0\) is \(1\) and
the \textbf{left-hand derivative} is \(-1\). So
\(\lim_{h\to0}\frac{\lvert h \rvert}{h}\) does not exist, and
\(\lvert x \rvert\) is not differentiable at \(0\); its graph has a
corner. Another example is \(x\sin(1/x)\) (with value \(0\) at \(0\)),
which is continuous at \(0\) but has no tangent there
(Exercise~\ref{exr-sin-one-over-x-tangent}).

Differentiability thus expresses precisely what we would intuitively
call \emph{smoothness} of a graph. How badly can it fail?

\begin{refremark}
\textbf{Question.} Does there exist a function that is continuous
everywhere but differentiable \emph{nowhere}?

\textbf{Answer.} Yes, surprisingly. The first published example is due
to Weierstrass (1872): \[
W(x) = \sum_{k=0}^\infty a^k\cos\big(b^k\pi x\big),
\] for suitable constants \(0<a<1\) and \(b>1\), for instance
\(a=\frac12\), \(b=3\). Each term is a smooth wave, and the terms get
smaller (\(a^k\to0\)) but oscillate faster and faster
(\(b^k\to\infty\)). The series converges fast enough for \(W\) to be
continuous, but the added oscillations create corners at every scale, so
that no tangent exists anywhere. Zooming in on the graph does not make
it look any straighter (Figure~\ref{fig-weierstrass}). Proving this
takes tools beyond this course.

\label{rem-weierstrass}

\end{refremark}

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{13_differentiation_rules_files/figure-pdf/fig-weierstrass-output-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-weierstrass}The Weierstrass function
\(W(x)=\sum_k 2^{-k}\cos(3^k\pi x)\) (partial sum with 13 terms). Right:
zooming in on the shaded window reveals the same kind of roughness.}

\end{figure}%

\section{Infinite Derivatives}\label{infinite-derivatives}

By the power rule, \(\frac{d}{dx}x^\alpha = \alpha x^{\alpha-1}\). For
some exponents the derivative is not defined at a point where the
original function is.

\begin{example}[]\protect\hypertarget{exm-cube-root}{}\label{exm-cube-root}

\hfill\break
Let \(f(x)=x^{1/3}=\sqrt[3]{x}\), with domain \(\mathbb{R}\)
(Remark~\ref{rem-roots}). For \(x\neq0\), the power rule predicts \[
f'(x) = \frac13\,x^{-2/3},
\] which you can verify directly in
Exercise~\ref{exr-cube-root-derivative}. This formula is undefined at
\(x=0\).

What happens at \(0\)? The graph has a \textbf{vertical tangent} there
(Figure~\ref{fig-cube-root}). Analytically, for \(h\neq0\), \[
\frac{f(0+h)-f(0)}{h} = \frac{h^{1/3}-0}{h} = h^{-2/3} = \frac{1}{\big(h^{1/3}\big)^2}
\longrightarrow \infty \qquad (h\to0).
\] The difference quotient grows beyond all bounds, from both sides, so
\(f\) is not differentiable at \(0\) even though it is continuous there.

\end{example}

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{13_differentiation_rules_files/figure-pdf/fig-cube-root-output-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-cube-root}The graph of \(y=\sqrt[3]{x}\), with
tangents of slope \(\frac13x^{-2/3}\). As \(x\to0\) from either side the
tangents become steeper without bound; at the origin the tangent is
vertical.}

\end{figure}%

One says that \(f\) has an \textbf{infinite derivative} at such a point.
Keep in mind that this is only a manner of speaking: the derivative in
the sense of Definition~\ref{def-derivative} does \emph{not} exist, and
the difference quotient merely increases beyond all bounds. We met the
same phenomenon, from one side only, for \(\sqrt x\) at \(0\)
(Example~\ref{exm-derivative-sqrt}).

\begin{exercise}[]\protect\hypertarget{exr-cube-root-derivative}{}\label{exr-cube-root-derivative}

Let \(x\neq 0\). Using the identity \(u^3-v^3 = (u-v)(u^2+uv+v^2)\) with
\(u=x_1^{1/3}\) and \(v=x^{1/3}\), show directly from
Definition~\ref{def-derivative} that \((x^{1/3})' = \frac13x^{-2/3}\).
Where does your argument break down for \(x=0\)?

\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
For \(x_1\neq x\), with \(u=x_1^{1/3}\), \(v=x^{1/3}\) we have
\(u\neq v\) and \(x_1-x=u^3-v^3\), so \[
\frac{x_1^{1/3}-x^{1/3}}{x_1-x} = \frac{u-v}{(u-v)(u^2+uv+v^2)} = \frac{1}{u^2+uv+v^2}.
\] As \(x_1\to x\), \(u\to v\) by continuity of the cube root, so the
denominator tends to \(3v^2 = 3x^{2/3}\). If \(x\neq0\) this is nonzero,
and the limit laws give \(\frac{1}{3x^{2/3}} = \frac13x^{-2/3}\). If
\(x=0\), then \(v=0\) and the denominator \(u^2\) tends to \(0\): the
quotient \(\frac{1}{u^2}\) is unbounded.
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}[]\protect\hypertarget{exr-cusp}{}\label{exr-cusp}

Let \(g(x) = x^{2/3} = \big(\sqrt[3]{x}\big)^2\). Compute the difference
quotient of \(g\) at \(0\) and describe what happens as \(h\to0^+\) and
as \(h\to0^-\). Sketch the graph near the origin.

\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
For \(h\neq0\),
\(\dfrac{g(h)-g(0)}{h} = \dfrac{h^{2/3}}{h} = h^{-1/3} = \dfrac{1}{\sqrt[3]{h}}\).
As \(h\to0^+\) this tends to \(+\infty\), and as \(h\to0^-\) it tends to
\(-\infty\). The graph has a \textbf{cusp} at the origin: both branches
meet the \(y\)-axis vertically, coming down from above on either side.
(C\&J call this curve the \emph{semicubical parabola}.)
\end{solution}

\section{What the Derivative Tells Us About a
Function}\label{what-the-derivative-tells-us-about-a-function}

If \(f'(x)>0\), the tangent at \((x,f(x))\) has positive slope, and we
expect \(f\) to be ``increasing at \(x\)''. If \(f'(x)<0\), we expect
\(f\) to be decreasing there. To make this precise, we have to talk
about intervals.

\begin{proposition}[Monotonicity and the sign of
\(f'\)]\protect\hypertarget{prp-monotone-derivative}{}\label{prp-monotone-derivative}

Let \(f\) be differentiable on an open interval \(I\).

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  If \(f\) is increasing on \(I\) (1), then \(f'(x)\ge 0\) for all
  \(x\in I\). If \(f\) is decreasing on \(I\), then \(f'(x)\le0\) for
  all \(x\in I\).
\item
  If \(f'(x)>0\) for all \(x\in I\), then \(f\) is strictly increasing
  on \(I\). If \(f'(x)<0\) for all \(x\in I\), then \(f\) is strictly
  decreasing on \(I\).
\end{enumerate}

\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
\leavevmode

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{(\arabic{enumi})}
\item
  Let \(f\) be increasing and \(x\in I\). For \(h>0\) (with
  \(x+h\in I\)), \(f(x+h)\ge f(x)\), so the difference quotient
  \(\frac{f(x+h)-f(x)}{h}\) is \(\ge0\). For \(h<0\),
  \(f(x+h)\le f(x)\): numerator and denominator are both \(\le 0\), and
  again the quotient is \(\ge0\). So every difference quotient is
  \(\ge 0\), and so is their limit \(f'(x)\) (compare the argument at
  the end of the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm-ivt}). The decreasing case is
  the same with the inequalities reversed.
\item
  This direction is deeper. It needs the \textbf{Mean Value Theorem} of
  differential calculus, and we will prove it then (C\&J §2.8.h--i).
\end{enumerate}

\end{proof}

\begin{refremark}
\leavevmode

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Statement (2) cannot be reversed: a strictly increasing function can
  have \(f'(x)=0\) at some points. For \(f(x)=x^3\), which is strictly
  increasing on \(\mathbb{R}\) (Example~\ref{exm-monotone}), we have
  \(f'(0)=0\). This is why (1) only gives \(f'\ge 0\), even for
  \emph{strictly} increasing \(f\).
\item
  What does \(f'(x)>0\) at a \emph{single} point \(x\) tell us? Since
  the difference quotient tends to \(f'(x)>0\), it is positive for all
  \(h\) with \(0<\lvert h \rvert<\delta\), for some \(\delta>0\). That
  is, \[
  f(x+h) > f(x) \text{ for } 0<h<\delta, \qquad f(x+h)<f(x) \text{ for } -\delta<h<0 .
  \] This is the precise meaning of ``\(f\) is increasing at \(x\)''. It
  compares values of \(f\) only with the value \emph{at \(x\)}, and does
  not mean that \(f\) is increasing on any interval around \(x\). (The
  function \(x+2x^2\sin(1/x)\), with value \(0\) at \(0\), has
  derivative \(1\) at \(0\) but is not increasing on any interval around
  \(0\); we will be able to check this once we have the product and
  chain rules.)
\end{enumerate}

\label{rem-monotone-derivative}

\end{refremark}

\begin{definition}[]\protect\hypertarget{def-critical-point}{}\label{def-critical-point}

\hfill\break
Let \(f\) be differentiable at \(x\). If \(f'(x)=0\), then \(f\) has a
\textbf{horizontal tangent} at \(x\), and \(x\) is called a
\textbf{critical point} of \(f\).

\end{definition}

For example, \(0\) is a critical point of \(x^2\), where the function
has its minimum, and of \(x^3\), where it has neither a minimum nor a
maximum.

Next, consider how the derivative itself changes. If \(f'\) is
increasing on an interval, the slope of \(f\) increases from left to
right: the graph \textbf{bends upward}. If \(f'\) is decreasing, the
graph bends downward (Figure~\ref{fig-shape-from-derivative}).

\begin{definition}[]\protect\hypertarget{def-concavity}{}\label{def-concavity}

\hfill\break
Let \(f\) be differentiable on an interval \(I\). Then \(f\) is

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{concave up} on \(I\) if \(f'\) is increasing on \(I\);
\item
  \textbf{concave down} on \(I\) if \(f'\) is decreasing on \(I\).
\end{itemize}

\end{definition}

For instance, \(x^2\) is concave up on \(\mathbb{R}\), since
\(f'(x)=2x\) is increasing. The function \(x^3\) has \(f'(x)=3x^2\),
which is decreasing on \((-\infty,0]\) and increasing on \([0,\infty)\):
so \(x^3\) is concave down on \((-\infty,0]\) and concave up on
\([0,\infty)\).

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{13_differentiation_rules_files/figure-pdf/fig-shape-from-derivative-output-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-shape-from-derivative}Left: \(f(x)=x^3-3x\) with
tangents. Right: \(f'(x)=3x^2-3\)
(Example~\ref{exm-polynomial-derivative}). Where \(f'>0\)
(\(\lvert x \rvert>1\)), \(f\) increases; where \(f'<0\), \(f\)
decreases; at the critical points \(x=\pm1\) the tangent is horizontal.
Where \(f'\) decreases (\(x<0\)), \(f\) is concave down; where \(f'\)
increases (\(x>0\)), \(f\) is concave up.}

\end{figure}%

\section{Summary}\label{summary-10}

\begin{itemize}
\item
  The difference quotient \(\frac{\Delta y}{\Delta x}\) is the
  \textbf{average rate of change} of \(f\); the derivative is the
  \textbf{instantaneous rate of change}. For a position \(y=f(t)\), the
  difference quotient is the \textbf{average velocity} and
  \(f'(t)=\dot f(t)\) is the \textbf{velocity} at time \(t\)
  (Figure~\ref{fig-average-velocity}). A freely falling body,
  \(f(t)=at^2\), has velocity \(2at\) (Example~\ref{exm-free-fall}).
\item
  \textbf{Basic derivatives}: \((mx+b)'=m\)
  (Example~\ref{exm-derivative-linear}); \((x^n)' = nx^{n-1}\) for all
  integers \(n\) (with \(x\neq0\) if \(n<0\)), by the factorization
  Equation~\ref{eq-power-difference}
  (Proposition~\ref{prp-power-rule-positive},
  Proposition~\ref{prp-power-rule-negative}); \((\sin x)'=\cos x\) and
  \((\cos x)'=-\sin x\) (Proposition~\ref{prp-derivative-sin-cos}),
  using \(\frac{\sin h}{h}\to1\) and \(\frac{\cos h-1}{h}\to0\)
  (Lemma~\ref{lem-trig-limits}).
\item
  When computing derivatives, keep \(\lim\) in every line until the
  limit is taken (Remark~\ref{rem-writing-limits}).
\item
  \textbf{Linearity}: \((f+g)'=f'+g'\) and \((cf)'=cf'\)
  (Proposition~\ref{prp-derivative-sum-rule},
  Proposition~\ref{prp-derivative-constant-multiple}), so every
  polynomial can be differentiated term by term
  (Example~\ref{exm-polynomial-derivative}). There is no such rule for
  products (Remark~\ref{rem-no-product-rule-derivative}).
\item
  \textbf{Differentiable implies continuous}
  (Theorem~\ref{thm-differentiable-continuous}), as long as the
  derivative is finite (Remark~\ref{rem-finite-derivative}). The
  converse fails: \(\lvert x \rvert\) has a corner at \(0\), with
  one-sided derivatives \(\pm1\), and there are even continuous
  functions that are nowhere differentiable
  (Remark~\ref{rem-weierstrass}).
\item
  A continuous function can have an \textbf{infinite derivative}, where
  the difference quotient is unbounded and the tangent is vertical, as
  for \(\sqrt[3]{x}\) at \(0\) (Example~\ref{exm-cube-root}).
\item
  \textbf{Shape from the derivative}: increasing \(f\) has \(f'\ge0\),
  and \(f'>0\) on an interval makes \(f\) strictly increasing (proof
  later, via the Mean Value Theorem)
  (Proposition~\ref{prp-monotone-derivative}); the converse fails for
  \(x^3\) at \(0\). Points with \(f'(x)=0\) are \textbf{critical points}
  (Definition~\ref{def-critical-point}). \(f\) is \textbf{concave
  up/down} where \(f'\) is increasing/decreasing
  (Definition~\ref{def-concavity}).
\end{itemize}

\chapter{Activity: Reading Derivatives from
Graphs}\label{activity-reading-derivatives-from-graphs}

\section{Background}\label{background-1}

For now we use the geometric meaning of the derivative: \(f'(x_0)\) is
the \textbf{slope of the tangent line} to the graph of \(f\) at the
point \((x_0, f(x_0))\). Reading off a slope from a graph means laying a
ruler along the curve at \(x_0\) and measuring rise over run, using the
grid squares. Doing this at every \(x\) produces a new function,
\(x \mapsto f'(x)\), with its own graph.

\section{Warm-up: a dictionary}\label{warm-up-a-dictionary}

\begin{exercise}[]\protect\hypertarget{exr-deriv-dictionary}{}\label{exr-deriv-dictionary}

Complete the right-hand column. Each row translates a feature of the
graph of \(f\) into a feature of the graph of \(f'\).

{\def\LTcaptype{none} % do not increment counter
\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.5000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.5000}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
If the graph of \(f\) \ldots{}
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
\ldots{} then the graph of \(f'\) \ldots{}
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
is increasing on an interval & ~ \\
is decreasing on an interval & ~ \\
has a horizontal tangent at \(x_0\) & ~ \\
is a straight line of slope \(m\) on an interval & ~ \\
bends upward (is concave up) on an interval & ~ \\
bends downward (is concave down) on an interval & ~ \\
changes from bending down to bending up (inflection point) at \(x_0\) &
~ \\
has a corner at \(x_0\) & ~ \\
\end{longtable}
}

\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
\leavevmode

{\def\LTcaptype{none} % do not increment counter
\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.5000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.5000}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
If the graph of \(f\) \ldots{}
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
\ldots{} then the graph of \(f'\) \ldots{}
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
is increasing on an interval & lies on or above the \(x\)-axis there \\
is decreasing on an interval & lies on or below the \(x\)-axis there \\
has a horizontal tangent at \(x_0\) & meets the \(x\)-axis at \(x_0\):
\(f'(x_0)=0\) \\
is a straight line of slope \(m\) on an interval & is the horizontal
line at height \(m\) there \\
bends upward (is concave up) on an interval & is increasing there \\
bends downward (is concave down) on an interval & is decreasing there \\
changes from bending down to bending up at \(x_0\) & has a local minimum
at \(x_0\) \\
has a corner at \(x_0\) & has no value at \(x_0\); it typically jumps
there \\
\end{longtable}
}

Note ``on or above'' in the first row, not ``above'': \(x \mapsto x^3\)
is strictly increasing, yet its tangent at \(0\) is horizontal. A zero
of \(f'\) does \emph{not} by itself signal a maximum or minimum.
Problems 2 and 4 contain exactly this situation.
\end{solution}

\section{\texorpdfstring{Part I: From \(f\) to
\(f'\)}{Part I: From f to f\textquotesingle{}}}\label{part-i-from-f-to-f}

In each problem, the top panel shows the graph of \(f\). Sketch the
graph of \(f'\) on the empty axes below it; the \(x\)-axes are aligned,
so you can transfer \(x\)-coordinates straight down.

\subsection{Problem 1}\label{problem-1}

\begin{figure}

\begin{exercise}[]\protect\hypertarget{exr-deriv-p1}{}\label{exr-deriv-p1}

~

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  Find all points where the tangent to \(f\) is horizontal. Mark the
  corresponding zeros of \(f'\) on the lower axes.
\item
  Between these points, decide whether \(f\) is increasing or
  decreasing, and hence whether the graph of \(f'\) lies above or below
  the \(x\)-axis.
\item
  Where is \(f\) \emph{steepest} while going down? What does that tell
  you about \(f'\) there?
\item
  Estimate slopes with a ruler and fill in the table. Plot the points on
  the lower axes.

\begin{solution}
The function is \(f(x) = \tfrac18(x^3 - 12x)\), so
\(f'(x) = \tfrac38(x^2-4)\), an upward parabola.

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Horizontal tangents at \(x=\pm2\) (local max at \(-2\), local min at
  \(2\)), so \(f'(\pm2)=0\).
\item
  \(f\) increases on \([-4,-2]\) and \([2,4]\) (\(f'\ge 0\)) and
  decreases on \([-2,2]\) (\(f'\le 0\)).
\item
  The steepest descent is at the inflection point \(x=0\), where \(f'\)
  has its minimum \(-1.5\).
\item
  \(f\) bends down on \([-4,0]\) and up on \([0,4]\), matching \(f'\)
  decreasing, then increasing.
\end{itemize}

\end{solution}

\subsection{Problem 2}\label{problem-2}

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{14_activity_derivative_graphs_files/figure-pdf/fig-deriv-p2-output-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-deriv-p2}Problem 2: the graph of \(f\) (top) and
axes for your sketch of \(f'\) (bottom).}

\end{figure}%

\begin{exercise}[]\protect\hypertarget{exr-deriv-p2}{}\label{exr-deriv-p2}

~

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  At \(x=-1\) the tangent to \(f\) is horizontal. Does \(f\) have a
  maximum or a minimum there? What does the graph of \(f'\) do near
  \(x=-1\) --- does it cross the \(x\)-axis or only touch it?
\item
  To the right of \(x=1\) the graph of \(f\) is a straight line. What is
  its slope, and what does \(f'\) look like there?
\item
  What happens at \(x = 1\)? Compare the slopes just to the left and
  just to the right of \(1\). Is there a sensible value for \(f'(1)\)?
  How do you show this in your sketch?
\item
  Sketch \(f'\) on the whole interval \([-3,3]\).
\end{enumerate}

\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
On \([-3,1]\), \(f(x) = \tfrac14(x+1)^3 + 1\), so
\(f'(x) = \tfrac34(x+1)^2\); on \([1,3]\), \(f(x) = 4-x\), so
\(f'(x) = -1\).

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{14_activity_derivative_graphs_files/figure-pdf/fig-deriv-p2-sol-output-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-deriv-p2-sol}Solution to Problem 2. The open circles
mark that \(f'(1)\) is undefined.}

\end{figure}%

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  At \(x=-1\) the tangent is horizontal, yet \(f\) keeps increasing on
  both sides: there is no extremum. Accordingly the graph of \(f'\)
  \textbf{touches} the \(x\)-axis at \(-1\) without crossing it. (A
  ``flat'' inflection point, as for \(x^3\) at \(0\).)
\item
  On \((1,3]\) the graph is a line of slope \(-1\), so \(f'\) is
  constantly \(-1\).
\item
  Just left of \(1\) the slope is close to \(3\); just right of \(1\) it
  is \(-1\). No single tangent line fits at the corner, so \(f'(1)\) is
  undefined, and the graph of \(f'\) \textbf{jumps} from \(3\) to
  \(-1\). We draw open circles at both ends. Note that \(f\) itself is
  continuous at \(1\): continuity does not guarantee a derivative (C\&J
  §2.8.e).
\end{itemize}

\end{solution}

\section{\texorpdfstring{Part II: From \(f'\) back to
\(f\)}{Part II: From f\textquotesingle{} back to f}}\label{part-ii-from-f-back-to-f}

Now the roles are reversed: the top panel shows the graph of the
\textbf{derivative} \(f'\), and your task is to sketch \(f\) itself.

\subsection{Problem 3}\label{problem-3}

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{14_activity_derivative_graphs_files/figure-pdf/fig-deriv-p3-output-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-deriv-p3}Problem 3: the graph of \(f'\) (top) and
two sets of axes for your sketches of \(f\).}

\end{figure}%

\begin{exercise}[]\protect\hypertarget{exr-deriv-p3}{}\label{exr-deriv-p3}

~

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Read off from the graph of \(f'\): on which intervals is \(f\)
  increasing or decreasing? Where does \(f\) have a local maximum or
  minimum? Where does \(f\) bend up, where does it bend down, and where
  is it a straight line (with what slope)?
\item
  Sketch \textbf{a} function \(f\) with this derivative on axes A.
\item
  Compare your sketch with your neighbor's. Are they the same? On axes
  A, sketch a \emph{second} function that also has the derivative shown,
  but is different from your first one. How are the two graphs related
  to each other?
\item
  Describe all functions whose derivative is the one shown. How much
  information about \(f\) is the graph of \(f'\) missing?
\item
  Now suppose you are also told that \[
  f(-2) = -1.
  \] Sketch \(f\) on axes B. Is there now only one possible graph? Read
  off approximate values of \(f(-4)\), \(f(0)\), \(f(2)\), \(f(3)\), and
  \(f(4)\) from your sketch.
\end{enumerate}

\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
\leavevmode

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{14_activity_derivative_graphs_files/figure-pdf/fig-deriv-p3-sol-output-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-deriv-p3-sol}Solution to Problem 3. Gray: several
functions with the given derivative. Red: the one with \(f(-2) = -1\).}

\end{figure}%

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \(f' < 0\) on \([-4,-2)\) and \((3,4]\), \(f'>0\) on \((-2,3)\): \(f\)
  decreases, then increases, then decreases, with a local minimum at
  \(x=-2\) and a local maximum at \(x=3\). Since \(f'\) increases on
  \([-4,0]\), \(f\) bends up there; on \([0,2]\), \(f'=2\) is constant,
  so \(f\) is a straight line of slope \(2\); on \([2,4]\), \(f'\)
  decreases, so \(f\) bends down.
\item
  A correct sketch has all the features from part 1; where it sits
  vertically is up to you.
\item
  Any two correct sketches differ by a \textbf{vertical shift}: the
  derivative only records slopes, and sliding a graph up or down changes
  no slope.
\item
  If \(f\) is one such function, the others are exactly the functions
  \(f + C\), \(C\) a constant. That every function with the same
  derivative arises this way is a real theorem, not just a picture: it
  says that a function with derivative \(0\) on an interval is constant,
  which follows from the mean value theorem (C\&J §2.8.h, §2.9.b). The
  graph of \(f'\) determines \(f\) only up to this one unknown number.
\item
  The condition \(f(-2) = -1\) pins down the constant: of all the
  vertical shifts, exactly one passes through the point \((-2,-1)\). The
  red graph is the unique answer, with \(f(-4)=1\), \(f(0)=1\),
  \(f(2)=5\), \(f(3)=6\), \(f(4)=5\). (Explicitly:
  \(f(x)=\tfrac12x^2+2x+1\) on \([-4,0]\), \(f(x)=2x+1\) on \([0,2]\),
  \(f(x)=-x^2+6x-3\) on \([2,4]\).)
\end{enumerate}

\end{solution}

\subsection{Problem 4}\label{problem-4}

Here \(f\) is continuous on \([-3,3]\) and differentiable everywhere
except at \(x=0\); its derivative is shown below.

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{14_activity_derivative_graphs_files/figure-pdf/fig-deriv-p4-output-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-deriv-p4}Problem 4: the graph of \(f'\) (top) and
two sets of axes for your sketches of \(f\). The open circles indicate
that \(f'(0)\) is undefined.}

\end{figure}%

\begin{exercise}[]\protect\hypertarget{exr-deriv-p4}{}\label{exr-deriv-p4}

~

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Read off the main features of \(f\). In particular: what kind of point
  is \(x=-1\)? What does the graph of \(f\) look like at \(x=0\)? Does
  \(f\) have a maximum or minimum at \(x=2\)?
\item
  On axes A, sketch two different functions with the derivative shown.
\item
  Now you are told that \[
  f(2) = 1.
  \] Sketch \(f\) on axes B. Read off approximate values of \(f(-3)\),
  \(f(-1)\), \(f(0)\), and \(f(3)\).
\end{enumerate}

\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
\leavevmode

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{14_activity_derivative_graphs_files/figure-pdf/fig-deriv-p4-sol-output-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-deriv-p4-sol}Solution to Problem 4. Gray: several
functions with the given derivative. Red: the one with \(f(2) = 1\).}

\end{figure}%

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \(f'\) changes sign from \(-\) to \(+\) at \(x=-1\): a local minimum.
  At \(x=0\) the slope jumps from \(1\) to \(3\): \(f\) has a
  \textbf{corner} there (the reverse of Problem 2). At \(x=2\), \(f'\)
  touches zero without changing sign: \(f\) has a horizontal tangent but
  keeps increasing --- no extremum. \(f\) bends up on \([-3,0]\) and on
  \([2,3]\), and bends down on \([0,2]\).
\item
  Again, any two answers differ by a vertical shift.
\item
  Only one shift passes through \((2,1)\): \(f(-3)=0.5\),
  \(f(-1)=-1.5\), \(f(0)=-1\), \(f(3)=1.25\). To reach the left half
  from the given point you must pass \emph{through} the corner, and this
  is where the continuity of \(f\) at \(0\) is used (see Going Further,
  question 3).
\end{enumerate}

\end{solution}

\section{Going further}\label{going-further}

\begin{exercise}[]\protect\hypertarget{exr-deriv-further}{}\label{exr-deriv-further}

~

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Change as area.} In Problem 3, compute the signed area between
  the graph of \(f'\) and the \(x\)-axis from \(x=-2\) to \(x=3\) (it is
  made of triangles and a rectangle). Compare it with \(f(3) - f(-2)\)
  from your sketch. Repeat for another pair of points. What do you
  conjecture?
\item
  \textbf{Why the real numbers matter.} Define \(h\) on the
  \emph{rational} numbers by \(h(x) = 0\) if \(x < \sqrt2\) and
  \(h(x) = 1\) if \(x > \sqrt2\). Convince yourself that at every
  rational \(x\), the graph of \(h\) is flat nearby, so its
  ``derivative'' is \(0\) everywhere on \(\mathbb{Q}\). Yet \(h\) is not
  constant. Which property of \(\mathbb{R}\) (from the first weeks of
  the course) rules this out for functions on a real interval?
\item
  \textbf{Is one value always enough?} In Problem 4, drop the assumption
  that \(f\) is continuous at \(0\), so \(f\) is only required to have
  the given derivative on \([-3,0)\) and on \((0,3]\). Does \(f(2) = 1\)
  still determine \(f\)? How many function values would you need?
\end{enumerate}

\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
\leavevmode

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  The area is \(2\) (triangle on \([-2,0]\)) \(+\ 4\) (rectangle on
  \([0,2]\)) \(+\ 1\) (triangle on \([2,3]\)) \(= 7\), and indeed
  \(f(3) - f(-2) = 6 - (-1) = 7\). The conjecture \[
  f(b) - f(a) = \int_a^b f'(x)\,dx
  \] is the \textbf{fundamental theorem of calculus} (C\&J §2.9). It
  explains the ambiguity in a second way: \(f'\) determines every
  \emph{difference} \(f(b)-f(a)\), but not the starting height.
\item
  On \(\mathbb{Q}\) the jump of \(h\) happens across a ``hole'' at
  \(\sqrt2\) that contains no input of \(h\). On \(\mathbb{R}\) there
  are no such holes: this is completeness (the nested interval
  principle). The proof that \(f'=0\) forces \(f\) constant goes through
  the mean value theorem, which in turn rests on completeness.
\item
  No.~On each of the two pieces \(f\) is determined up to its own
  constant, and without continuity the two constants are independent:
  the left half of the graph can be shifted up or down on its own. You
  need one value from each side of \(0\) --- for instance \(f(2)\) and
  \(f(-1)\). The principle is: one value per \emph{interval} on which
  \(f\) is differentiable.
\end{enumerate}

\end{solution}

\chapter{The Mean Value Theorem}\label{the-mean-value-theorem}

\section{Local and Global
Information}\label{local-and-global-information}

The derivative \[
f'(x) = \lim_{h\to0}\frac{f(x+h)-f(x)}{h}
\] is a \textbf{local} quantity: it only depends on the values of \(f\)
in an arbitrarily small neighborhood of \(x\). A difference quotient \[
\frac{f(b)-f(a)}{b-a},
\] on the other hand, compares the values of \(f\) at two points that
may be far apart, so it reflects a \textbf{global} property of \(f\).
The Mean Value Theorem connects the two. It is the most important tool
for deriving \emph{global} properties of a function from \emph{local}
properties of its derivative.

\textbf{Geometric picture.} The difference quotient
\(\frac{f(b)-f(a)}{b-a}\) is the slope of the \textbf{secant} through
the points \((a,f(a))\) and \((b,f(b))\) of the graph of \(f\). Imagine
shifting this secant parallel to itself, away from the graph. At some
point \(\xi\) between \(a\) and \(b\) the shifted line will just touch
the graph without crossing it: it becomes the \textbf{tangent} at
\(\xi\). (One such point is the point of the graph that is farthest from
the secant.) Since the tangent is parallel to the secant, the two have
the same slope: \[
f'(\xi) = \frac{f(b)-f(a)}{b-a}.
\]

\begin{exercise}[]\protect\hypertarget{exr-mvt-fails}{}\label{exr-mvt-fails}

Try to draw the graph of a function on \([a,b]\) for which there is
\textbf{no} point \(\xi\in(a,b)\) at which the tangent is parallel to
the secant. Is your function continuous? Does it have a tangent at every
point?

\end{exercise}

\section{The Mean Value Theorem}\label{the-mean-value-theorem-1}

\begin{theorem}[Mean Value
Theorem]\protect\hypertarget{thm-mvt}{}\label{thm-mvt}

Suppose \(f\) is continuous on \([a,b]\) and differentiable on
\((a,b)\). Then there exists \(\xi\) with \(a<\xi<b\) and \[
f'(\xi) = \frac{f(b)-f(a)}{b-a}.
\]

\end{theorem}

Equivalently, \(f(b)-f(a) = f'(\xi)\,(b-a)\). The theorem asserts only
that such a \(\xi\) \emph{exists}; it says nothing about where in
\((a,b)\) it lies, and there may be several such points. Note also that
\(f\) must be continuous on the \emph{closed} interval, but
differentiability is only required on the \emph{open} interval: the
derivative need not exist at the endpoints.

Why do we need to assume that \(f'(x)\) exists for \textbf{all}
\(x\in(a,b)\)? A single point of non-differentiability is enough to
break the theorem.

\begin{example}[]\protect\hypertarget{exm-abs-no-mvt}{}\label{exm-abs-no-mvt}

\hfill\break
Let \(f(x) = \lvert x \rvert\) on \([-1,1]\). Then \(f\) is continuous
on \([-1,1]\), and the secant has slope \[
\frac{f(1)-f(-1)}{1-(-1)} = \frac{1-1}{2} = 0 .
\] But \(f'(x) = -1\) for \(x<0\) and \(f'(x)=1\) for \(x>0\), while
\(f'(0)\) does not exist (the left- and right-sided limits of the
difference quotient are \(-1\) and \(1\)). So there is no
\(\xi\in(-1,1)\) with \(f'(\xi)=0\). The graph has a corner at \(0\),
and no tangent is parallel to the secant.

\end{example}

\begin{example}[Driving]\protect\hypertarget{exm-driving}{}\label{exm-driving}

\hfill\break
Suppose you drive for \(2\) hours and end up \(120\) miles from where
you started. Let \(s(t)\) be your distance from the starting point at
time \(t\in[0,2]\). Assume that your movement is continuous and that
your velocity \(s'(t)\) exists at all times. By Theorem~\ref{thm-mvt},
there is a moment \(\xi\in(0,2)\) at which your velocity was \[
s'(\xi) = \frac{s(2)-s(0)}{2-0} = \frac{120-0}{2-0} = 60 \text{ mph}.
\] Here the numerator is a distance and the denominator a time. In
particular, you cannot claim to have driven at less than \(50\) mph the
whole time. On the other hand, the theorem does not tell us \emph{when}
you were going exactly \(60\) mph.

\end{example}

\section{Rolle's Theorem}\label{rolles-theorem}

We prove the Mean Value Theorem by first establishing the special case
of a horizontal secant.

\begin{theorem}[Rolle's
Theorem]\protect\hypertarget{thm-rolle}{}\label{thm-rolle}

Suppose \(f\) is continuous on \([a,b]\) and differentiable on
\((a,b)\), and \(f(a)=f(b)=0\). Then there exists \(\xi\) with
\(a<\xi<b\) and \[
f'(\xi) = 0.
\]

\end{theorem}

This is the Mean Value Theorem with a horizontal tangent: if the graph
of \(f\) meets the \(x\)-axis at \(a\) and at \(b\), it must have a
horizontal tangent somewhere in between.

The proof rests on the following key fact, which we have used before
(see Remark~\ref{rem-darboux-informal}) and will prove later.

\begin{theorem}[Extreme Value
Theorem]\protect\hypertarget{thm-evt}{}\label{thm-evt}

A continuous function on a closed interval \([a,b]\) assumes a global
maximum and a global minimum. That is, there exist \(M\) and \(m\) such
that \[
m \le f(x) \le M \qquad\text{for all } x\in[a,b],
\] and there are points \(x_m, x_M\in[a,b]\) with \(f(x_m)=m\) and
\(f(x_M)=M\).

\end{theorem}

With this, we can prove Theorem~\ref{thm-rolle}.

\begin{proof}
Let \(m\) and \(M\) be the minimum and maximum of \(f\) on \([a,b]\)
(Theorem~\ref{thm-evt}). Since \(f(a)=f(b)=0\), we must have \[
m \le 0 \le M .
\]

\emph{Case \(m = M = 0\).} Then \(f(x)=0\) for all \(x\in[a,b]\). Every
difference quotient of \(f\) is \(0\), so \(f'(\xi) = 0\) for every
\(\xi\in(a,b)\), and we are done.

\emph{Case \(M>0\).} There exists \(\xi\in[a,b]\) with \(f(\xi)=M\).
Since \(f(a)=f(b)=0<M\), \(\xi\) is an \textbf{interior point}:
\(\xi\in(a,b)\). Since \(f(x)\le M\) for all \(x\in[a,b]\), we have \[
f(\xi+h) - f(\xi) \le 0
\] for all \(h\) with \(\xi+h\in[a,b]\), in particular for all small
enough \(\lvert h \rvert\). Hence \[
\frac{f(\xi+h)-f(\xi)}{h} \le 0 \quad\text{for } h>0,
\qquad\qquad
\frac{f(\xi+h)-f(\xi)}{h} \ge 0 \quad\text{for } h<0 .
\] Since \(f\) is differentiable at \(\xi\), the limit \[
\lim_{h\to0}\frac{f(\xi+h)-f(\xi)}{h}
\] exists, no matter from which direction \(h\to0\). Letting \(h\to0\)
through positive values, the limit is \(\le0\); letting \(h\to0\)
through negative values, it is \(\ge0\) (a limit of nonpositive numbers
is nonpositive; compare the argument at the end of the proof of
Theorem~\ref{thm-ivt}). Since the left-sided and the right-sided limit
agree (Definition~\ref{def-one-sided-limits}), we must have \[
f'(\xi) = \lim_{h\to0}\frac{f(\xi+h)-f(\xi)}{h} = 0 .
\]

\emph{Case \(M=0\), \(m<0\).} The same argument, applied at a point
\(\xi\) where \(f\) attains its minimum \(m\) (or the previous case
applied to \(-f\)), gives \(f'(\xi)=0\).
\end{proof}

\begin{refremark}
The proof shows more than Rolle's theorem: if \(f\) attains a maximum or
a minimum at an \textbf{interior} point \(\xi\) of an interval, and
\(f\) is differentiable at \(\xi\), then \(f'(\xi)=0\). The assumption
\(f(a)=f(b)=0\) was only used to guarantee that an extremum occurs in
the interior.

\label{rem-interior-extremum}

\end{refremark}

\section{Proof of the Mean Value
Theorem}\label{proof-of-the-mean-value-theorem}

To deduce Theorem~\ref{thm-mvt} from Theorem~\ref{thm-rolle}, we
subtract the secant from \(f\). Define \[
\varphi(x) := f(x) - f(a) - \frac{f(b)-f(a)}{b-a}\,(x-a).
\] The secant through \((a,f(a))\) and \((b,f(b))\) is the line
\(y = f(a) + \frac{f(b)-f(a)}{b-a}(x-a)\), so \(\varphi(x)\) is the
\textbf{vertical distance} between the point \((x,f(x))\) on the graph
and the point of the secant above (or below) \(x\).

\begin{proof}
Plugging in the endpoints, \[
\varphi(a) = f(a) - f(a) - 0 = 0,
\qquad
\varphi(b) = f(b) - f(a) - \frac{f(b)-f(a)}{b-a}(b-a) = 0 .
\] The function \(\varphi\) is of the form \[
\varphi(x) = f(x) + c\,x + d,
\qquad\text{with constants}\quad
c = -\frac{f(b)-f(a)}{b-a}, \quad d = -f(a) + \frac{f(b)-f(a)}{b-a}\,a .
\] So \(\varphi\) is continuous on \([a,b]\)
(Corollary~\ref{cor-continuity-algebra}), and since \[
\frac{\varphi(x+h)-\varphi(x)}{h} = \frac{f(x+h)-f(x)}{h} + c ,
\] it is differentiable on \((a,b)\) with \[
\varphi'(x) = f'(x) + c .
\] By Theorem~\ref{thm-rolle} there exists \(\xi\) with \(a<\xi<b\) and
\(\varphi'(\xi)=0\). Hence \[
f'(\xi) = -c = \frac{f(b)-f(a)}{b-a} .
\]
\end{proof}

\begin{refremark}
Applying Theorem~\ref{thm-mvt} to a function with \(f(a)=f(b)\) gives
\(f'(\xi)=0\). So Rolle's theorem holds more generally whenever
\(f(a)=f(b)\), not only when both values are \(0\).

\label{rem-rolle-general}

\end{refremark}

\section{Application: Lipschitz
Continuity}\label{application-lipschitz-continuity}

\begin{definition}[]\protect\hypertarget{def-lipschitz}{}\label{def-lipschitz}

\hfill\break
A function \(f\) is \textbf{Lipschitz continuous} on an interval \(I\)
if there is a constant \(L\ge0\) such that \[
\lvert f(x)-f(y) \rvert \le L\,\lvert x-y \rvert \qquad\text{for all } x,y\in I .
\]

\end{definition}

In other words, all difference quotients \(\frac{f(x)-f(y)}{x-y}\) of
\(f\) on \(I\) are bounded in absolute value by \(L\): the secant slopes
of \(f\) cannot become arbitrarily steep. A Lipschitz continuous
function is continuous: given \(\varepsilon>0\), the choice
\(\delta=\varepsilon/L\) (any \(\delta\) if \(L=0\)) works in
Definition~\ref{def-continuity} at every point.

We say \(f\) is \textbf{continuously differentiable} on \([a,b]\) if
\(f'\) exists on \([a,b]\) (as a one-sided derivative at the endpoints)
and \(f'\) is continuous on \([a,b]\).

\begin{proposition}[]\protect\hypertarget{prp-c1-lipschitz}{}\label{prp-c1-lipschitz}

\hfill\break
Any function \(f\) that is continuously differentiable on \([a,b]\) is
Lipschitz continuous on \([a,b]\).

\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Since \(f'\) is continuous on \([a,b]\), it is bounded there by
Theorem~\ref{thm-evt}: there is \(L\) with \[
\lvert f'(x) \rvert \le L \qquad\text{for all } x\in[a,b].
\] Now let \(a\le x<y\le b\). Then \(f\) is continuous on \([x,y]\) and
differentiable on \((x,y)\), so by Theorem~\ref{thm-mvt} there is
\(\xi\in(x,y)\) with \[
\lvert f(y)-f(x) \rvert = \lvert f'(\xi)\,(y-x) \rvert = \lvert f'(\xi) \rvert\,\lvert y-x \rvert \le L\,\lvert y-x \rvert .
\]
\end{proof}

The proof only used that \(f'\) is bounded on \((a,b)\) (and that \(f\)
is continuous on \([a,b]\)), so this weaker assumption is already
enough.

\begin{example}[Not every continuous function is Lipschitz
continuous]\protect\hypertarget{exm-cube-root-not-lipschitz}{}\label{exm-cube-root-not-lipschitz}

\hfill\break
Let \(f(x) = \sqrt[3]{x}\) on \([-1,1]\). This function is continuous.
But for \(x\neq0\), \[
\frac{f(x)-f(0)}{x-0} = \frac{x^{1/3}}{x} = x^{-2/3},
\] which is \textbf{not bounded} near \(0\): for \(x = \frac1{n^3}\) it
equals \(n^2\). So no constant \(L\) can satisfy
\(\lvert f(x)-f(0) \rvert\le L\lvert x-0 \rvert\) for all
\(x\in[-1,1]\). This does not contradict
Proposition~\ref{prp-c1-lipschitz}: \(f\) is not differentiable at \(0\)
--- the graph has a vertical tangent there, and
\(f'(x) = \frac13 x^{-2/3}\) is unbounded near \(0\).

\end{example}

\section{Summary}\label{summary-11}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  The \textbf{Mean Value Theorem} (Theorem~\ref{thm-mvt}): if \(f\) is
  continuous on \([a,b]\) and differentiable on \((a,b)\), then
  \(f'(\xi) = \frac{f(b)-f(a)}{b-a}\) for some \(\xi\in(a,b)\).
  Geometrically, some tangent is parallel to the secant. It turns local
  information (the derivative) into global information (difference
  quotients). For example, an average speed of \(60\) mph forces a
  moment of speed exactly \(60\) mph (Example~\ref{exm-driving}).
\item
  Differentiability at \textbf{every} interior point is needed:
  \(\lvert x \rvert\) on \([-1,1]\) has a corner and no horizontal
  tangent (Example~\ref{exm-abs-no-mvt}).
\item
  \textbf{Rolle's Theorem} (Theorem~\ref{thm-rolle}) is the special case
  \(f(a)=f(b)=0\). Its proof uses the \textbf{Extreme Value Theorem}
  (Theorem~\ref{thm-evt}): \(f\) attains a maximum or minimum at an
  interior point \(\xi\), and there the right-sided difference quotients
  are \(\le0\) and the left-sided ones \(\ge0\) (or vice versa), so
  \(f'(\xi)=0\) (Remark~\ref{rem-interior-extremum}).
\item
  The Mean Value Theorem follows by applying Rolle's theorem to
  \(\varphi(x) = f(x) - \big(\text{secant
  at } x\big)\), which vanishes at \(a\) and \(b\) and has
  \(\varphi' = f' - \frac{f(b)-f(a)}{b-a}\).
\item
  \textbf{Application:} a continuously differentiable function on
  \([a,b]\) is \textbf{Lipschitz continuous},
  \(\lvert f(x)-f(y) \rvert\le L\lvert x-y \rvert\) with \(L\) a bound
  for \(\lvert f' \rvert\) (Proposition~\ref{prp-c1-lipschitz}).
  Continuous functions need not be Lipschitz: \(\sqrt[3]{x}\) has
  unbounded difference quotients at \(0\)
  (Example~\ref{exm-cube-root-not-lipschitz}).
\end{itemize}

\chapter{The Derivative as Linear
Approximation}\label{the-derivative-as-linear-approximation}

\section{The Tangent Line as an
Approximation}\label{the-tangent-line-as-an-approximation}

Near the point \((a,f(a))\), the graph of a differentiable function is
very close to its tangent line. Since lines are easy to evaluate, we can
use the tangent to \textbf{approximate} the values \(f(a+h)\) for small
\(h\) (Figure~\ref{fig-linear-approximation}).

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{16_linear_approximation_files/figure-pdf/fig-linear-approximation-output-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-linear-approximation}The tangent at \((a,f(a))\)
approximates the graph near \(a\). Moving from \(a\) to \(a+h\), the
tangent rises by \(f'(a)\,h\). The value \(L(a+h)\) on the tangent
differs from the actual value \(f(a+h)\) by the error
\(\varepsilon h\).}

\end{figure}%

To use the tangent, we need its equation. The tangent at \((a,f(a))\) is
the line through the \textbf{point} \((a,f(a))\) with \textbf{slope}
\(f'(a)\) (Definition~\ref{def-derivative}). In point-slope form, \[
y - f(a) = f'(a)\,(x-a).
\]

\begin{definition}[]\protect\hypertarget{def-linear-approximation}{}\label{def-linear-approximation}

\hfill\break
Let \(f\) be differentiable at \(a\). The \textbf{linear approximation}
(or \textbf{linearization}) of \(f\) about \(x=a\) is the function \[
L(x) = f(a) + f'(a)\,(x-a).
\]

\end{definition}

To approximate \(f\) close to \(a\), plug in \(a+h\) for \(x\): \[
f(a+h) \approx L(a+h) = f(a) + f'(a)\,(a+h-a) = f(a) + f'(a)\cdot h .
\] So to estimate \(f\) near \(a\), we only need two numbers: the value
\(f(a)\) and the derivative \(f'(a)\).

\begin{example}[]\protect\hypertarget{exm-linear-approx-quadratic}{}\label{exm-linear-approx-quadratic}

\hfill\break
Let \(f(x) = x^2+1\) and \(a=2\). Then \(f'(x) = 2x\), so \[
f(2) = 5, \qquad f'(2) = 4 .
\] With \(h=0.1\), \[
f(2.1) \approx L(2.1) = 5 + 4\cdot 0.1 = 5.4 .
\] The true value is \(f(2.1) = 2.1^2+1 = 4.41+1 = 5.41\). The
approximation is off by \(0.01\), and it is an \textbf{underestimate}.

\end{example}

\begin{exercise}[]\protect\hypertarget{exr-linear-approx-cubic}{}\label{exr-linear-approx-cubic}

Let \(f(x) = x^3-2x+4\). Approximate \(f(1.1)\) using \(f(1)\) and
\(f'(1)\), and compare with the true value.

\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
We have \(f(1) = 1-2+4 = 3\) and \(f'(x) = 3x^2-2\), so \(f'(1) = 1\).
The linear approximation about \(1\) is \[
L(x) = f(1) + f'(1)\,(x-1) = 3 + (x-1),
\] and \[
f(1.1) \approx L(1.1) = 3 + (1.1-1) = 3 + 0.1 = 3.1 .
\] The true value is \(f(1.1) = 1.331 - 2.2 + 4 = 3.131\). Again the
approximation is an underestimate, this time by \(0.031\).
\end{solution}

\begin{exercise}[]\protect\hypertarget{exr-why-underestimate}{}\label{exr-why-underestimate}

In Example~\ref{exm-linear-approx-quadratic} and
Exercise~\ref{exr-linear-approx-cubic}, the linear approximation
\emph{underestimates} the true value. Why? Sketch the graph of \(f\)
near \(a\) together with its tangent.

\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
In both cases \(f'\) is increasing near \(a\): \(f'(x)=2x\) for the
first function and \(f'(x) = 3x^2-2\) (increasing for \(x\ge0\)) for the
second. So \(f\) is \textbf{concave up} near \(a\)
(Definition~\ref{def-concavity}): the graph bends upward, away from the
tangent, and therefore lies \textbf{above} the tangent line. The tangent
values \(L(a+h)\) are then too small. In
Figure~\ref{fig-linear-approximation} the function is concave down, the
graph lies below the tangent, and the approximation is an overestimate.
We prove this precisely in Proposition~\ref{prp-tangent-below-graph}.
\end{solution}

\section{Estimating the Error}\label{estimating-the-error}

How far off is \(L(a+h)\), relative to \(h\)? We write the error as a
multiple of \(h\): \[
f(a+h) = L(a+h) + \underbrace{\varepsilon h}_{\text{error term}}
       = f(a) + f'(a)\,h + \varepsilon h ,
\] where \(\varepsilon\) depends on \(h\). Solving for \(\varepsilon\),
\[
\frac{f(a+h)-f(a)}{h} = f'(a) + \varepsilon.
\]

\begin{refremark}
The left-hand side is the difference quotient of \(f\) at \(a\), which
tends to \(f'(a)\) as \(h\to0\). So \[
\varepsilon= \frac{f(a+h)-f(a)}{h} - f'(a) \longrightarrow 0 \qquad (h\to0) ,
\] Conversely, suppose \(f(a+h) = f(a) + c\,h + \varepsilon h\) for some
number \(c\), with \(\varepsilon\to0\) as \(h\to0\). Then
\(\frac{f(a+h)-f(a)}{h} = c + \varepsilon\to c\), so \(f\) is
differentiable at \(a\) with \(f'(a)=c\). In other words, \(f\) is
differentiable at \(a\) exactly when \(f\) can be approximated near
\(a\) by a linear function with an error that is small \emph{compared to
\(h\)}. This is how C\&J phrase the definition of the derivative in
§2.8.j.

\label{rem-epsilon-differentiability}

\end{refremark}

This tells us that the error goes to \(0\) faster than \(h\), but not
\emph{how much} faster. To estimate \(\varepsilon\), suppose \(h>0\) and
\(f\) is differentiable on an open interval containing \([a,a+h]\). By
the Mean Value Theorem (Theorem~\ref{thm-mvt}), there is \(\xi\) with
\(a<\xi<a+h\) and \[
\frac{f(a+h)-f(a)}{h} = f'(\xi) .
\] Comparing with the expression above gives
\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-epsilon-mvt}{
\varepsilon= f'(\xi) - f'(a) .
}\end{equation}

So the error depends on how much the derivative changes between \(a\)
and \(\xi\). This settles Exercise~\ref{exr-why-underestimate}.

\begin{proposition}[Tangent lines of concave-up
functions]\protect\hypertarget{prp-tangent-below-graph}{}\label{prp-tangent-below-graph}

Let \(f\) be differentiable on an open interval \(I\) containing \(a\),
and concave up on \(I\) (Definition~\ref{def-concavity}). Then the graph
of \(f\) lies on or above its tangent at \(a\): \[
f(x) \ge L(x) = f(a) + f'(a)(x-a) \qquad\text{for all } x\in I .
\]

\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Let \(x=a+h\in I\). For \(h=0\) both sides equal \(f(a)\). For \(h>0\),
Equation~\ref{eq-epsilon-mvt} gives
\(f(a+h) - L(a+h) = \varepsilon h = \big(f'(\xi)-f'(a)\big)h\) with
\(a<\xi<a+h\). Since \(f'\) is increasing, \(f'(\xi)\ge f'(a)\), so this
is \(\ge0\).

For \(h<0\), apply Theorem~\ref{thm-mvt} on \([a+h,a]\) instead:
\(\frac{f(a+h)-f(a)}{h} = \frac{f(a)-f(a+h)}{a-(a+h)} = f'(\xi)\) for
some \(\xi\) with \(a+h<\xi<a\), and again
\(f(a+h)-L(a+h) = \big(f'(\xi)-f'(a)\big)h\). Now \(f'(\xi)\le f'(a)\)
and \(h<0\), so the product is again \(\ge0\).
\end{proof}

For a concave-down function the inequality is reversed, and the linear
approximation overestimates.

\subsection{Bounding the error with the second
derivative}\label{bounding-the-error-with-the-second-derivative}

How can we bound \(f'(\xi) - f'(a)\)? If \(f'\) is itself
differentiable, we can use the Mean Value Theorem again, this time for
\(f'\). The derivative of \(f'\) is the \textbf{second derivative} of
\(f\), \[
f''(x) = (f')'(x) .
\] For example, \(f(x)=x^2+1\) has \(f'(x)=2x\) and \(f''(x)=2\), and
\(f(x)=x^3-2x+4\) has \(f'(x)=3x^2-2\) and \(f''(x) = 6x\).

Applying Theorem~\ref{thm-mvt} to \(f'\) on \([a,\xi]\), we find
\(\eta\) with \(a<\eta<\xi\) and \[
f'(\xi) - f'(a) = f''(\eta)\,(\xi - a) .
\] Since \(0<\xi-a<h\), it follows that \[
\lvert \varepsilon \rvert = \underbrace{\lvert \xi-a \rvert}_{\le h}\cdot\underbrace{\lvert f''(\eta) \rvert}_{\le M} \le h\cdot M ,
\] where \(M\) is any bound for \(\lvert f'' \rvert\) on the interval
\([a,a+h]\). Multiplying by \(h\), we obtain a bound for the error term.

\begin{theorem}[Error of the linear
approximation]\protect\hypertarget{thm-linear-approx-error}{}\label{thm-linear-approx-error}

Let \(f\) be twice differentiable on an open interval containing \(a\)
and \(a+h\), and let \(M\) be a bound for \(\lvert f'' \rvert\) on the
closed interval between \(a\) and \(a+h\): \[
\lvert f''(x) \rvert \le M \qquad\text{for all } x \text{ between } a \text{ and } a+h.
\] Then \[
\lvert f(a+h) - L(a+h) \rvert = \lvert \varepsilon h \rvert \le M\,h^2 .
\]

\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For \(h>0\) this is the computation above. (The second application of
Theorem~\ref{thm-mvt} is allowed because \(f'\) is differentiable, hence
continuous by Theorem~\ref{thm-differentiable-continuous}, on an open
interval containing \([a,\xi]\).) For \(h<0\), the same argument on the
interval \([a+h,a]\) gives
\(\lvert \varepsilon \rvert\le\lvert h \rvert\cdot M\), hence
\(\lvert \varepsilon h \rvert\le M h^2\). For \(h=0\) there is nothing
to prove.
\end{proof}

For small \(h\), the bound \(M h^2\) is \textbf{much smaller} than the
linear term \(f'(a)\,h\), unless \(f'(a)=0\): their ratio is
\(\frac{M h^2}{\lvert f'(a) \rvert\,\lvert h \rvert} = \frac{M}{\lvert f'(a) \rvert}\lvert h \rvert\),
which goes to \(0\) with \(h\).

\begin{example}[]\protect\hypertarget{exm-error-bounds}{}\label{exm-error-bounds}

\hfill\break
We check Theorem~\ref{thm-linear-approx-error} against the two earlier
examples, with \(h=0.1\).

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  For \(f(x)=x^2+1\) and \(a=2\), we have \(\lvert f'' \rvert=2\), so
  \(M=2\) and the error is at most \(2\cdot(0.1)^2 = 0.02\). The actual
  error is \(0.01\). In fact, \(f(2+h) = 5+4h+h^2\), so the error is
  exactly \(h^2\).
\item
  For \(f(x)=x^3-2x+4\) and \(a=1\), we have \(f''(x)=6x\), so
  \(\lvert f'' \rvert\le 6.6\) on \([1,1.1]\). The error is at most
  \(6.6\cdot(0.1)^2 = 0.066\). The actual error is \(0.031\). In fact,
  \(f(1+h) = 3 + h + 3h^2 + h^3\), so the error is exactly \(3h^2+h^3\).
\end{itemize}

In both cases, the bound is about twice the actual error.

\end{example}

\begin{refremark}
The examples suggest that the constant \(M\) in
Theorem~\ref{thm-linear-approx-error} is not optimal. Indeed, one can
show the sharper bound \(\lvert \varepsilon h \rvert\le \frac12 M h^2\),
which is attained for \(x^2+1\) (where \(M=2\) and the error is
\(h^2\)). C\&J mention this improvement in §2.8.j and derive it later,
as part of Taylor's theorem.

\label{rem-better-constant}

\end{refremark}

\begin{exercise}[]\protect\hypertarget{exr-why-important}{}\label{exr-why-important}

Why is Theorem~\ref{thm-linear-approx-error} important?

\end{exercise}

\begin{solution}
A few reasons:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{The error is quadratic in \(h\).} Making \(h\) ten times
  smaller makes the error bound a hundred times smaller. For
  \(f(x)=x^3-2x+4\) at \(a=1\), the actual errors \(3h^2+h^3\) are \[
  0.031 \ (h=0.1), \qquad 0.000301\ (h=0.01), \qquad 0.000003001\ (h=0.001) .
  \] The linear term \(f'(1)\,h = h\), on the other hand, only shrinks
  by a factor of ten each time. So for small \(h\), almost all of the
  change \(f(a+h)-f(a)\) is captured by the linear term.
\item
  \textbf{It is a guarantee.} We usually use the linear approximation
  when we \emph{cannot} easily compute \(f(a+h)\). The theorem tells us
  how good the approximation is without knowing the true value: we only
  need a bound for \(f''\). For example, for \(f(x)=\sin x\) and \(a=0\)
  we get \(\sin(0.1)\approx \sin 0 + \cos 0\cdot 0.1 = 0.1\). Since
  \(f''(x) = -\sin x\) and \(\lvert \sin x \rvert\le x\le 0.1\) on
  \([0,0.1]\), the error is at most \(0.1\cdot(0.1)^2 = 0.001\). (The
  true value is \(\sin(0.1) = 0.09983\ldots\), an error of about
  \(0.00017\).)
\item
  \textbf{It makes step-by-step methods work.} Roughly speaking: in
  \emph{Euler's method}, a function is approximated by following tangent
  lines over many small steps of size \(h\). Over an interval of fixed
  length we need about \(1/h\) steps, each with an error of order
  \(h^2\). So the total error is of order \(\frac1h\cdot h^2 = h\),
  which goes to \(0\) as \(h\to0\). If the error per step were only
  \emph{proportional} to \(h\), the errors would add up to something
  that does not shrink.
\end{enumerate}

\end{solution}

\section{Summary}\label{summary-12}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  The \textbf{linear approximation} of \(f\) about \(a\) is the tangent
  line \(L(x) = f(a) + f'(a)(x-a)\)
  (Definition~\ref{def-linear-approximation}). For \(x=a+h\) it gives
  \(f(a+h)\approx f(a) + f'(a)\,h\), using only \(f(a)\) and \(f'(a)\).
  For example, \(2.1^2+1\approx 5.4\) (true value \(5.41\))
  (Example~\ref{exm-linear-approx-quadratic}).
\item
  Writing the error as \(\varepsilon h\), so that
  \(f(a+h) = f(a) + f'(a)\,h + \varepsilon h\), differentiability of
  \(f\) at \(a\) means exactly that \(\varepsilon\to0\) as \(h\to0\)
  (Remark~\ref{rem-epsilon-differentiability}).
\item
  By the \textbf{Mean Value Theorem}, \(\varepsilon= f'(\xi)-f'(a)\) for
  some \(\xi\) between \(a\) and \(a+h\)
  (Equation~\ref{eq-epsilon-mvt}). Consequently, the tangent of a
  \textbf{concave-up} function lies below its graph, and the linear
  approximation underestimates
  (Proposition~\ref{prp-tangent-below-graph}). For a concave-down
  function it overestimates.
\item
  Applying the Mean Value Theorem a second time, to \(f'\), gives the
  \textbf{error bound} \(\lvert f(a+h)-L(a+h) \rvert\le M h^2\), where
  \(M\) bounds \(\lvert f'' \rvert\) between \(a\) and \(a+h\)
  (Theorem~\ref{thm-linear-approx-error}). The error is quadratic in
  \(h\), much smaller than the linear term \(f'(a)\,h\) for small \(h\)
  (unless \(f'(a)=0\)). The sharper bound \(\frac12 Mh^2\) will come
  later (Remark~\ref{rem-better-constant}).
\end{itemize}

\chapter{Activity: Linear Approximation and Euler's
Method}\label{activity-linear-approximation-and-eulers-method}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, arc=.35mm, bottomrule=.15mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, colback=white, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, coltitle=black, left=2mm, leftrule=.75mm, opacityback=0, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, title={About this activity}, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm]

An in-class, pencil-and-paper worksheet. It builds on the linear
approximation from lecture, \[
L(x) = f(a) + f'(a)(x-a),
\] the tangent line to the graph of \(f\) at \(x=a\). Here we use it in
a new way: to estimate values of a function we do \emph{not} have a
formula for, but whose derivative we know through a \textbf{differential
equation}.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Background: Differential
Equations}\label{background-differential-equations}

Consider the equation \[
\frac{dy}{dx} = -2y .
\] This is an example of a \textbf{differential equation}. A
\textbf{solution} of this differential equation is a function
\(y = f(x)\) that makes the equation true, i.e.~\(f'(x) = -2f(x)\) for
all \(x\).

Notice that the equation does not tell us what \(f\) is. It tells us
something more indirect: at any point \((x, y)\) on the graph of a
solution, the slope of the tangent line is \(-2y\). If we know
\emph{one} point on the graph, that is exactly what we need to build a
linear approximation there.

\section{Step 1: A worked example}\label{step-1-a-worked-example}

Suppose we want a solution \(y = f(x)\) with the \textbf{initial data}
\(f(0) = 10\), and we wish to estimate \(f(0.1)\).

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Since we know \(f(0)\), we use the linear approximation about
  \(a = 0\): \[
  L(x) = f(0) + f'(0)(x - 0).
  \]
\item
  Use the initial data and the differential equation to compute
  \(f'(0)\). At \(x = 0\) we have \(y = f(0) = 10\), so \[
  f'(0) = \left.\frac{dy}{dx}\right|_{x=0} = -2(10) = -20.
  \]
\item
  Use \(f(0)\) and \(f'(0)\) to build the linear approximation about
  \(a = 0\): \[
  L(x) = 10 - 20x.
  \]
\item
  Use it to estimate \(f(0.1)\): \[
  f(0.1) \approx L(0.1) = 10 - 20(0.1) = 8.
  \]
\end{enumerate}

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{17_activity_euler_method_files/figure-pdf/fig-euler-first-step-output-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-euler-first-step}One step of Euler's method for
\(dy/dx = -2y\), \(f(0) = 10\): follow the tangent line at \((0, 10)\)
for a horizontal distance \(0.1\).}

\end{figure}%

We now know \(f(0) = 10\) and (approximately) \(f(0.1) = 8\). Nothing
stops us from repeating the process: use the differential equation to
get the slope at \(x = 0.1\), build the linear approximation about
\(a = 0.1\), and use it to estimate \(f(0.2)\). And so on. This method
of solving a differential equation numerically, one small tangent-line
step at a time, is called \textbf{Euler's method}.

\begin{exercise}[]\protect\hypertarget{exr-activity-euler-steps}{}\label{exr-activity-euler-steps}

~

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Using \(f(0.1) \approx 8\), find the linear approximation about
  \(a = 0.1\) and use it to estimate \(f(0.2)\).
\item
  Take one more step to estimate \(f(0.3)\).
\item
  Do you notice a pattern? Find a formula for the Euler estimate of
  \(f(x)\) if we go from \(0\) to \(x\) in \(n\) equal steps of size
  \(h = x/n\).
\end{enumerate}

\end{exercise}

\section{Step 2: Exponential growth}\label{step-2-exponential-growth}

Let \(N(t)\) denote the mass of a population of bacteria, in milligrams
(mg), at time \(t\) (in days). Assume that the per capita growth rate is
\(10\%\) per day: \[
\frac{1}{N}\frac{dN}{dt} = 0.1, \qquad\text{or}\qquad \frac{dN}{dt} = 0.1\,N.
\] Suppose we observe that at time \(t = 10\) days the mass of the
population is \(300\) mg, so that \(N(10) = 300\).

\begin{exercise}[]\protect\hypertarget{exr-activity-exponential-growth}{}\label{exr-activity-exponential-growth}

~

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Find the linear approximation for \(N(t)\) about \(a = 10\): \[
  L(t) = N(10) + N'(10)(t - 10).
  \]
\item
  Use your linear approximation to estimate the mass of the population
  when \(t = 10.5\).
\end{enumerate}

\end{exercise}

\section{Step 3: Logistic growth}\label{step-3-logistic-growth}

Let \(N(t)\) again denote the mass of a population of bacteria, in mg,
at time \(t\) (in days). This time the population is modeled by a
\textbf{logistic growth model} \[
\frac{dN}{dt} = \frac{1}{2}\,N\left(1 - \frac{N}{1000}\right).
\] Suppose we observe that at time \(t = 20\) days the mass of the
population is \(500\) mg, so that \(N(20) = 500\).

\begin{exercise}[]\protect\hypertarget{exr-activity-logistic-growth}{}\label{exr-activity-logistic-growth}

~

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Find the linear approximation for \(N(t)\) about \(a = 20\): \[
  L(t) = N(20) + N'(20)(t - 20).
  \]
\item
  Use your linear approximation to estimate the mass of the population
  when \(t = 20.1\).
\end{enumerate}

\end{exercise}

\section{Step 4: Pollution in a lake}\label{step-4-pollution-in-a-lake}

Let \(P(t)\) denote the amount of phosphorus pollution in a lake, in
tons, \(t\) months after monitoring has begun. Assume that the amount of
pollution is modeled by the differential equation \[
\frac{dP}{dt} = 20 - \frac{1}{5}\,P .
\] This says that pollution flows into the lake at a constant rate of
\(20\) tons per month and flows out at a rate proportional to the
current amount \(P\), with proportionality constant \(\tfrac15\), i.e.
\(20\%\) per month. Suppose we observe that at time \(t = 3\) months the
amount of phosphorus pollution is \(40\) tons, so that \(P(3) = 40\).

\begin{exercise}[]\protect\hypertarget{exr-activity-pollution}{}\label{exr-activity-pollution}

~

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Find the linear approximation for \(P(t)\) about \(a = 3\): \[
  L(t) = P(3) + P'(3)(t - 3).
  \]
\item
  Use your linear approximation to estimate the amount of phosphorus
  pollution in the lake one month later, at \(t = 4\).
\end{enumerate}

\end{exercise}

\section{Over or under?}\label{over-or-under}

In each of the three models, you can decide whether your estimate is too
high or too low \emph{without} knowing a formula for the solution, just
by reading the differential equation.

\begin{exercise}[]\protect\hypertarget{exr-activity-over-under}{}\label{exr-activity-over-under}

For each of Steps 2--4: as \(t\) increases past the base point \(a\),
does the solution increase or decrease? And does the \textbf{slope}
given by the differential equation then get larger or smaller? Use this
to decide whether your linear approximation is an \textbf{overestimate}
or an \textbf{underestimate}. Which of the three estimates do you expect
to be most accurate, and why?

\end{exercise}
